 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Explain what Python is and what "interpreted, high-level, dynamically typed" means in practice.
+- Describe the execution model: source code, bytecode, and the interpreter.
+- Recognize where Python is a strong choice and where it is not.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Python is a general-purpose programming language used for automation, data processing, web services, scientific computing, and security analysis. Python 3 is the version family to learn. CPython compiles source to bytecode internally and executes it; beginners commonly run `.py` source through the Python interpreter.
+Python is a general-purpose programming language created by Guido van Rossum and first released in 1991. Three properties define how it feels to work with:
 
-## Core Concepts
+- **Interpreted.** You run a source file directly and the Python interpreter executes it line by line. There is no separate compile-and-link step in your workflow, so the feedback loop from "edit" to "run" is seconds.
+- **High-level.** You work with concepts like "read this file" or "sort this list" instead of managing memory addresses. The interpreter handles allocation and garbage collection for you.
+- **Dynamically typed.** Types belong to values, not to variable names. A name can refer to an integer on one line and a string later, and type errors surface at runtime rather than at compile time.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+These traits make Python fast to write and easy to read, at the cost of raw speed compared to compiled languages like C or Rust.
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## How Python Actually Runs Your Code
+
+When you run `python script.py`, the interpreter (almost always CPython, the reference implementation written in C) does two things:
+
+1. **Compiles** your source into *bytecode*, a lower-level set of instructions. You can see this cached in `__pycache__` directories as `.pyc` files.
+2. **Executes** the bytecode in the CPython virtual machine, a loop that reads one bytecode instruction at a time and performs it.
+
+```text
+script.py  --(compile)-->  bytecode (.pyc)  --(CPython VM)-->  output
+```
+
+This is why Python is "interpreted" but still has a compile step: the compilation is automatic and in-memory, not a separate command you run.
+
+You can inspect the bytecode yourself with the `dis` module:
 
 ```python
-print("Hello, Yaoguai!")
+import dis
+
+def greet(name):
+    return "Hello, " + name
+
+dis.dis(greet)
+```
+
+```text
+  2           2 LOAD_CONST               1 ('Hello, ')
+              4 LOAD_FAST                0 (name)
+              6 BINARY_ADD
+              8 RETURN_VALUE
+```
+
+You will never need to read bytecode for daily work, but knowing it exists explains error messages, `.pyc` files, and why "compiling Python" questions come up in interviews.
+
+## The REPL: Your Scratchpad
+
+Running `python` with no file opens the interactive interpreter (the REPL: Read, Evaluate, Print, Loop). It evaluates each line immediately and prints the result:
+
+```text
+$ python3
+Python 3.12.3 (main, ...)
+>>> 2 + 3
+5
+>>> "port " + str(8080)
+'port 8080'
+>>> len("yaoguai")
+7
 ```
-Output: `Hello, Yaoguai!`
 
-`print()` displays a value. Quoted text is a string. Python's readable syntax makes it approachable, but the language also provides powerful file, process, and networking APIs.
+Use the REPL constantly while learning: test one expression, check a method's behavior, explore a module with `dir()` and `help()`. Exit with `exit()` or Ctrl-D (Ctrl-Z then Enter on Windows).
 
-## Practical Exercise
+## Where Python Excels, and Where It Does Not
 
-Create `hello.py`, print two messages, and run it with `python hello.py` (Windows may use `py hello.py`; Linux may use `python3 hello.py`).
+Python is a strong default for:
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+- **Automation and scripting** - file processing, log parsing, renaming batches, scheduled jobs.
+- **Security work** - quick network tools, protocol experiments, exploit proofs of concept, glue between other tools. Most of the security ecosystem (pwntools, scapy, impacket) is Python.
+- **Web backends** - Django, Flask, and FastAPI power large production systems.
+- **Data analysis and machine learning** - pandas, NumPy, and PyTorch are the industry standard stack.
+
+It is a weaker choice for:
+
+- **CPU-bound number crunching** in pure Python (solved by calling C extensions, which is what NumPy does).
+- **Mobile apps and games**, where other toolchains dominate.
+- **Hard real-time systems**, where garbage collection pauses are unacceptable.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Thinking "interpreted" means there is no error checking until the line runs. Syntax errors are caught at the compile-to-bytecode step, before anything executes.
+- Confusing the `python` and `python3` commands. On many systems `python` is Python 2 or missing entirely; this course always means Python 3.
+- Editing a file, then running a different file by accident and wondering why nothing changed.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Python is interpreted, high-level, and dynamically typed; CPython compiles your source to bytecode and executes it in a VM.
+- The REPL gives instant feedback and is the fastest way to test an idea.
+- Python trades raw speed for development speed, which is usually the right trade for scripts, tools, and backends.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+Do these in your own terminal. Try each task before opening the solution.
+
+**Exercise 1.** Start the REPL. Evaluate an arithmetic expression, a string expression, and call a built-in function. Then exit.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```text
+$ python3
+>>> 17 * 24
+408
+>>> "bandit" * 2
+'banditbandit'
+>>> abs(-42)
+42
+>>> exit()
+```
+
+Any correct expressions count. The point is to get comfortable typing into `>>>` and leaving again.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** In the REPL, run `import this` and read the output. Pick one line of the Zen of Python and write down what you think it means.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```text
+>>> import this
+The Zen of Python, by Tim Peters
+
+Beautiful is better than ugly.
+Explicit is better than implicit.
+...
+```
+
+For example, "Explicit is better than implicit" means code should say what it does in plain sight rather than relying on hidden behavior, so the next reader does not have to guess.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Use `dis.dis()` on a one-line function you write yourself, as shown above. Find the `RETURN_VALUE` instruction in the output.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import dis
+
+def double(x):
+    return x * 2
+
+dis.dis(double)
+```
+
+```text
+  2           2 LOAD_FAST                0 (x)
+              4 LOAD_CONST               1 (2)
+              6 BINARY_MULTIPLY
+              8 RETURN_VALUE
+```
+
+The exact opcode names vary slightly between Python versions; finding `RETURN_VALUE` at the end is the goal.
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**What are the two stages CPython performs when you run a script?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+It compiles the source to bytecode, then executes that bytecode in the CPython virtual machine.
+</details>
+
+**In Python, do types belong to variables or to values?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+To values. A name is just a label and can be rebound to an object of a different type at any time.
+</details>
+
+**You type `python` and get "command not found", but Python is installed. What is the most likely fix?**
 
-## Further Practice
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-Modify the example, explain each line in your own words, and link the exercise to the next module.
+Use `python3` instead. Many systems do not link the plain `python` name to Python 3.
+</details>

-# Why Python for Cybersecurity
+# Python for Cybersecurity
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Explain why Python became the default scripting language in security work.
+- Name the categories of security tasks Python handles well, with one concrete example each.
+- State the rules that keep practice legal and safe.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Python is useful for repeatable defensive workflows: parsing logs, comparing file hashes, inventorying directories, inspecting authorized HTTP endpoints, and automating lab tasks. The script's purpose and authorization determine whether an activity is appropriate.
+Ask a working penetration tester what they write quick tools in and the answer is almost always Python. The reasons are practical:
 
-## Core Concepts
+- **Speed of writing.** In a security engagement, the tool you need often has to exist *now*. A port scanner, a log parser, or a payload encoder is an afternoon of Python, not a week of C.
+- **Batteries included.** The standard library already speaks HTTP, TCP, UDP, JSON, CSV, regular expressions, file compression, and process control. Many useful tools need no installation at all.
+- **The ecosystem.** Scapy crafts packets, pwntools drives exploit development, impacket speaks Windows network protocols, and Volatility (memory forensics) and sqlmap are Python too. Standing on this ecosystem means most "new" tools are 50 lines, not 5,000.
+- **Readable under pressure.** Incident response at 3 AM needs code you can still read. Python's plain syntax helps.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## What Security Work Looks Like in Python
 
-## Syntax and Example
+**Reconnaissance and networking.** Sockets, DNS, and HTTP are a few lines each:
 
-| Task | Useful tools |
-|---|---|
-| Parse logs | strings, dictionaries, `Counter` |
-| File integrity | `hashlib`, file handling |
-| HTTP inspection | `requests` |
-| TCP checks | `socket` |
-| Inventory | `pathlib`, `os` |
+```python
+import socket
 
-## Practical Exercise
+host = "scanme.nmap.org"
+for port in (22, 80, 443):
+    s = socket.socket()
+    s.settimeout(1.5)
+    result = s.connect_ex((host, port))
+    print(port, "open" if result == 0 else "closed")
+    s.close()
+```
 
-Choose one workflow. Identify its input, output, required Python features, and one safety boundary. Use only supplied data or systems you are authorized to test.
+```text
+22 open
+80 open
+443 closed
+```
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+`connect_ex` returns 0 when the connection succeeds, so this prints which ports answer. Module 15 covers sockets in depth.
+
+**Log analysis and forensics.** Reading millions of lines, extracting the ones that matter, and counting them:
+
+```python
+failed = 0
+with open("/var/log/auth.log") as log:
+    for line in log:
+        if "Failed password" in line:
+            failed += 1
+print("Failed logins:", failed)
+```
+
+**Exploitation and CTF work.** Sending exact bytes, encoding payloads, and scripting interactions with a target service. CTF platforms and tools like pwntools assume Python fluency.
+
+**Automation.** The unglamorous majority of security work: checking 200 hosts for a stale certificate, verifying backup permissions, diffing firewall exports. Scripts do it consistently and leave a record.
+
+## Rules of Engagement
+
+The techniques in this course are the same ones used in authorized assessments, and the same ones prosecuted when used without permission. The rules are not optional:
+
+1. **Only test systems you own or have written permission to test.** "Written" means a contract, scope document, or the published terms of a bug bounty or practice platform.
+2. **Practice targets exist so you never need to touch anything real.** OverTheWire (Bandit), TryHackMe, HackTheBox, PicoCTF, and `scanme.nmap.org` all explicitly invite practice.
+3. **Handle data you encounter responsibly.** A script that reads credentials, tokens, or personal data has the same obligations as a person reading them.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Copying an exploit script and running it against a random IP "to see if it works." That is unauthorized access in most jurisdictions, regardless of intent.
+- Assuming a tool is safe because it is popular; read any script before running it with your credentials or against your network.
+- Writing attack tooling with no logging, so you cannot reconstruct what it did afterward.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Python dominates security scripting because it is fast to write, has a huge ecosystem, and stays readable.
+- Networking, log analysis, exploitation, and automation are the four big use categories; this course builds toward all of them.
+- Authorization is the line between practice and a crime. Use the practice platforms, never arbitrary targets.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Run the port-check script above against `scanme.nmap.org` (a host Nmap provides for exactly this purpose). Add port 8080 to the list.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import socket
+
+host = "scanme.nmap.org"
+for port in (22, 80, 443, 8080):
+    s = socket.socket()
+    s.settimeout(1.5)
+    result = s.connect_ex((host, port))
+    print(port, "open" if result == 0 else "closed")
+    s.close()
+```
+
+```text
+22 open
+80 open
+443 closed
+8080 closed
+```
+
+Results can vary over time; the mechanism is what matters.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Write a script that counts how many lines of a text file contain the word "error" (case-insensitive). Test it on any log or text file you have.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+count = 0
+with open("app.log") as f:
+    for line in f:
+        if "error" in line.lower():
+            count += 1
+print("Lines containing 'error':", count)
+```
+
+Create a small `app.log` with a few mixed-case "Error"/"error" lines first so you can verify the count by hand.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Look up the terms of service or rules page of one practice platform (OverTheWire, TryHackMe, or PicoCTF). Write down in one sentence what you are allowed to attack there.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+For OverTheWire: you may attack only the game servers on the ports they publish, using the accounts the game gives you; attacking their other infrastructure or other players is forbidden. The exact wording matters more than this paraphrase; read it yourself.
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why is `connect_ex` convenient for scanning compared to `connect`?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+It returns an error code instead of raising an exception, so a closed port does not crash the loop; you just check for 0.
+</details>
+
+**Name the four broad categories of security work this lesson lists for Python.**
+
+<details>

+Networking/reconnaissance, log analysis and forensics, exploitation and CTF work, and automation.

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.

 ## Introduction
 
-Install Python 3 from python.org on Windows or from your Linux distribution's package manager. Verify the interpreter before configuring an editor. PATH is the list of directories a shell searches when resolving commands.
+Every example in this course assumes Python 3.10 or newer. Python 2 reached end of life in January 2020 and must never be used for new work; if a tutorial shows `print "hello"` without parentheses, it is Python 2 and you should close it.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Linux
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Most distributions ship Python 3 preinstalled. Check first:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```text
+$ python3 --version
+Python 3.12.3
+```
+
+If it is missing or too old, install through your package manager:
+
+```bash
+# Debian / Ubuntu
+sudo apt update && sudo apt install python3 python3-pip python3-venv
+
+# Fedora / RHEL
+sudo dnf install python3 python3-pip
 
-Windows PowerShell:
-```powershell
-py --version
-python --version
+# Arch
+sudo pacman -S python python-pip
 ```
-Linux:
+
+The `python3-venv` package matters: Debian and Ubuntu split it out, and without it you cannot create virtual environments (module 02 lesson 3).
+
+## macOS
+
+The cleanest route is Homebrew:
+
 ```bash
-python3 --version
-sudo apt update
-sudo apt install python3 python3-venv python3-pip
+brew install python@3.12
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+This provides `python3` and `pip3` without touching the Python that macOS keeps for its own tools. Never modify or delete the system Python at `/usr/bin/python3`.
+
+## Windows
+
+Install from python.org or the Microsoft Store. The python.org installer has one critical checkbox:
 
-Install Python 3 and record the command that works. If `python` is not found, try `py` on Windows or `python3` on Linux, reopen the terminal, and check PATH.
+- **Check "Add python.exe to PATH"** on the first screen. If you skip it, the terminal will not find Python.
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Windows also installs the `py` launcher, which is often the most reliable command:
+
+```text
+C:\> py --version
+Python 3.12.4
+C:\> py -3.11 --version
+Python 3.11.9
+```
+
+`py` can select among installed versions, which is why many Windows users prefer it over `python`.
+
+## Verify the Installation
+
+Three commands should all work:
+
+```text
+$ python3 --version
+Python 3.12.3
+$ python3 -c "import sys; print(sys.executable)"
+/usr/bin/python3
+$ python3 -m pip --version
+pip 24.0 from /usr/lib/python3/dist-packages/pip (python 3.12)
+```
+
+The second tells you *which* interpreter you are actually running - essential once several versions coexist. The third confirms pip belongs to the same Python; always run pip as `python3 -m pip` rather than a bare `pip`, so packages land in the interpreter you think they do.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Installing Python but forgetting "Add to PATH" on Windows, then concluding the install failed.
+- Using `pip install` and `python3 script.py` from different installations, so the script cannot find the package it just installed. `python3 -m pip` prevents this.
+- Running scripts with `python` and silently getting Python 2 on an older system.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Python 3.10+ is required; Python 2 is dead and should be avoided entirely.
+- Verify with `--version`, `sys.executable`, and `python3 -m pip --version` so you know which interpreter and which pip you have.
+- On Windows, tick "Add to PATH" and learn the `py` launcher.
+

+/usr/bin/python3
+```
+
+One entry means a single system Python. Multiple entries (for example `/usr/local/bin/python3` before `/usr/bin/python3`) mean several installs; the first one wins because the shell searches PATH left to right.

 ## Introduction

+The REPL is for exploring; scripts are for keeping. A Python script is a plain text file, conventionally ending in `.py`, executed with the interpreter:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```bash
+python3 hello.py
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.

-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+print("Hello from a file")
+name = input("Your name: ")
+print(f"Welcome, {name}")
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 hello.py
+Hello from a file
+Your name: Youssef
+Welcome, Youssef
+```
+
+Three things to notice: `print` writes to standard output, `input()` reads a line from standard input and always returns a string, and the f-string `f"Welcome, {name}"` inserts the variable into the text.
+
+## Passing Arguments on the Command Line
+
+Real tools take arguments: `nmap scanme.nmap.org`, `grep error app.log`. Python exposes them through `sys.argv`, a list of strings where element 0 is the script name:
 
 ```python
-message = "Learning Python"
-print(message)
+import sys
+
+print("Script:", sys.argv[0])
+print("Arguments:", sys.argv[1:])
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 echo_args.py alpha beta
+Script: echo_args.py
+Arguments: ['alpha', 'beta']
+```
+
+Everything in `sys.argv` is a string, even numbers. Convert explicitly and validate before use:
+
+```python
+import sys
+
+if len(sys.argv) != 2:
+    print(f"Usage: {sys.argv[0]} PORT", file=sys.stderr)
+    sys.exit(2)
+
+try:
+    port = int(sys.argv[1])
+except ValueError:
+    print("PORT must be a number", file=sys.stderr)
+    sys.exit(2)
+
+print(f"Would connect to port {port}")
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 port.py 8080
+Would connect to port 8080
+$ python3 port.py
+Usage: port.py PORT
 ```
-Run with `py hello.py` on Windows or `python3 hello.py` on Linux. Start an interactive prompt with `py` or `python3`; leave it with `exit()`.
 
-## Practical Exercise
+For anything beyond one or two arguments, the standard library's `argparse` module builds real command-line interfaces with `--flags`, help text, and validation. You will meet it in later projects.
 
-Calculate `17 * 4` interactively, then save and run a script. Use `pwd` (Linux) or `Get-Location` (PowerShell) to inspect the current directory.
+## Exit Codes
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+When a script ends, it reports an integer *exit code* to the shell: 0 means success, anything else means failure. Other programs (cron, CI, shell scripts) rely on this:
+
+```bash
+python3 check_host.py && echo "host is up"
+```
+
+`sys.exit(2)` above sets the code explicitly; an unhandled exception exits with 1 and prints a traceback. Check the last code in bash with `echo $?`.
+
+## Executable Scripts on Linux and macOS
+
+Add a shebang line and the execute bit to run a script directly:
+
+```python
+#!/usr/bin/env python3
+print("runs like a program")
+```
+
+```bash
+chmod +x tool.py
+./tool.py
+```
+
+`#!/usr/bin/env python3` tells the kernel which interpreter to use; `env` finds `python3` on PATH so the script works across machines.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Forgetting `sys.argv` values are strings and comparing `sys.argv[1] > 1024`, which compares text lexicographically and misbehaves on numbers like `"80"`.
+- Indexing `sys.argv[1]` without checking `len(sys.argv)` first, producing an unhelpful `IndexError` for the user.
+- Using `input()` for passwords. It echoes to the screen; use `getpass.getpass()` for secrets.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Scripts are plain `.py` files run with `python3 file.py`; `print` and `input` cover basic I/O.
+- `sys.argv` carries command-line arguments as strings; validate count and type, and print usage to stderr.
+- Exit code 0 means success; set nonzero codes with `sys.exit()` so shells can react.
+

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.

-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Explain what a virtual environment is and why every project gets one.
+- Create, activate, and deactivate a venv on any platform.
+- Install, pin, and reproduce dependencies with pip and `requirements.txt`.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-A virtual environment isolates dependencies for one project. `pip` installs third-party packages; use `python -m pip` to associate pip with the selected interpreter.
+Python packages install into the interpreter's `site-packages` directory. With a single global interpreter, two projects that need different versions of the same library collide, and `sudo pip install` can break your OS's own Python tools. The standard fix is a **virtual environment** (venv): a folder containing a private Python interpreter and its own `site-packages`, so each project's dependencies are isolated and disposable.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Create and Activate
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```bash
+python3 -m venv .venv
+```
 
-## Syntax and Example
+This creates a `.venv` folder in the current directory (the name is a convention; `venv` and `env` are also common). Nothing is active yet. Activation rewrites your shell's PATH so `python` and `pip` resolve inside the environment:
 
-Windows:
-```powershell
-py -m venv .venv
-.\.venv\Scripts\Activate.ps1
-```
-Linux:
 ```bash
-python3 -m venv .venv
+# Linux / macOS
 source .venv/bin/activate
+
+# Windows PowerShell
+.venv\Scripts\Activate.ps1
+
+# Windows cmd
+.venv\Scripts\activate.bat
+```
+
+The prompt changes to show the environment name:
+
+```text
+(.venv) $ which python
+/home/you/project/.venv/bin/python
+```
+
+Deactivate with `deactivate`. Deleting the `.venv` folder removes the environment completely; your code is untouched. If PowerShell refuses the activate script, run `Set-ExecutionPolicy -Scope CurrentUser RemoteSigned` once.
+
+## Installing with pip
+
+Inside an active venv, install packages without `sudo`:
+
+```text
+(.venv) $ python -m pip install requests
+Collecting requests
+  Downloading requests-2.32.3 ...
+Successfully installed certifi-2024.8.30 charset-normalizer-3.4.0 idna-3.10 requests-2.32.3 urllib3-2.2.3
+```
+
+Useful daily commands:
+
+```bash
+python -m pip install requests        # latest version
+python -m pip install requests==2.32.3  # exact version (pinning)
+python -m pip install --upgrade requests
+python -m pip show requests           # version, location, dependencies
+python -m pip list                    # everything installed here
+python -m pip uninstall requests
 ```
-Install `requests` with `python -m pip install requests`. Save dependencies using `python -m pip freeze > requirements.txt`; restore with `python -m pip install -r requirements.txt`. Deactivate with `deactivate`.
 
-## Practical Exercise
+## Reproducing an Environment with requirements.txt
+
+A project should record exactly what it needs so anyone (including future you and your CI) can rebuild it:
+

+python -m pip freeze > requirements.txt
+```

+- Installing into the global Python because the venv was created but never activated. Check for the `(.venv)` prefix or `which python` before every `pip install`.

-# Syntax and Variables
+# Syntax, Variables, and Assignment
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Read and write Python's block structure: indentation, colons, and comments.
+- Assign and reassign variables, and follow Python's naming rules and conventions.
+- Understand assignment as name-binding, not copying.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Python uses indentation to define blocks. Use four spaces consistently. A variable name refers to an object; assignment binds a name to a value. Python is dynamically typed. Use descriptive `snake_case` names; uppercase names indicate constants by convention.
+Python's most visible design decision is that **indentation is syntax**. Where other languages wrap blocks in braces, Python uses a colon followed by a consistently indented block:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+attempts = 3
+
+if attempts > 2:
+    print("many attempts")   # inside the if-block
+    print("still inside")    # same indentation level
+print("always runs")         # back at the top level
+```
+
+```text
+many attempts
+still inside
+always runs
+```
+
+The convention is **4 spaces per level**, and mixing tabs with spaces is an error in Python 3. Any decent editor inserts 4 spaces when you press Tab; configure yours once and never think about it again. Comments start with `#` and run to the end of the line.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## Variables Are Names Bound to Objects
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Assignment with `=` binds a name to an object. It does not copy anything:
 
 ```python
-# A useful comment
-username = "analyst"
-attempts = 3
-attempts += 1
-MAX_RETRIES = 5
+ports = [22, 80, 443]
+services = ports          # both names refer to the SAME list
+services.append(8080)
+print(ports)              # [22, 80, 443, 8080] - changed too!
+```
+
+```text
+[22, 80, 443, 8080]
+```
 
-if attempts < MAX_RETRIES:
-    print(f"{username}: {attempts}")
+`services` is not a copy; it is a second label on the same list. Mutating through one name is visible through the other. To get an independent copy of a list, use `ports.copy()` or `ports[:]`.
+
+This model explains a classic surprise:
+
+```python
+a = 500
+b = a
+b = b + 1
+print(a)   # 500 - ints are immutable, so b = b + 1 binds b to a NEW object
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Numbers, strings, and tuples are immutable, so "changing" them actually creates a new object and rebinds the name. Lists and dictionaries are mutable, so methods like `.append()` change the object in place. Knowing which is which prevents most aliasing bugs.
 
-Create variables for a filename, byte count, and Boolean file-exists flag. Update the byte count and print a formatted summary.
+## Naming Rules and Conventions
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Rules (the interpreter enforces these):
+
+- Letters, digits, and underscore; cannot start with a digit.
+- Case-sensitive: `port` and `Port` are different names.
+- Reserved keywords (`if`, `for`, `class`, `import`, ...) cannot be names.
+
+Conventions (the community enforces these, and tools like linters check them):
+
+- `snake_case` for variables and functions: `failed_logins`, `parse_log`.
+- `UPPER_SNAKE` for constants: `MAX_RETRIES = 5`.
+- `CapWords` for classes (module 08+).
+- Never shadow built-ins: naming a variable `list`, `str`, or `id` breaks that built-in for the rest of the scope and produces deeply confusing errors.
+
+Multiple assignment and swapping are pleasantly direct:
+
+```python
+host, port = "scanme.nmap.org", 80
+a, b = b, a          # swap without a temp variable
+```
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Inconsistent indentation, especially after pasting code. Python raises `IndentationError` or, worse, silently runs the line in the wrong block.
+- Expecting `b = a` to copy a list, then "fixing" data through one name and corrupting it through the other.
+- Long lines of "clever" one-liners. Python culture values the boring, readable version.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Indentation defines blocks: 4 spaces, never mixed with tabs; `#` starts a comment.
+- Assignment binds a name to an object; mutating a shared object is visible through every name bound to it.
+- Use `snake_case`, avoid shadowing built-ins, and prefer readable code over clever code.
+

+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why does modifying a list through one variable sometimes change what another variable "contains"?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Both names are bound to the same list object. Assignment never copies; only explicit copying (`.copy()`, slicing) creates an independent object.
+</details>
+
+**What is wrong with naming a variable `list`?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+It shadows the built-in `list` type within that scope, so later calls like `list("abc")` fail with a confusing error.
+</details>

+**How many spaces per indentation level does the Python community standardize on?**
 
-## Further Practice

+## Integers and Floats
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Python integers are **arbitrary precision**: they never overflow, they just get slower when astronomically large:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+big = 2 ** 100
+print(big)
+```
+
+```text
+1267650600228229401496703205376
+```
+
+Floats are 64-bit IEEE 754, like `double` in C, and carry the classic rounding artifacts:
+
+```python
+print(0.1 + 0.2)
+print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3)
+```
+
+```text
+0.30000000000000004
+False
+```
+
+Never compare floats for equality; compare within a tolerance (`math.isclose(a, b)`), and use the `decimal` module for money.
+
+Division is the operator that surprises people most:
 
 ```python
-raw = input("Count: ")
-try:
-    count = int(raw)

+print(7 // 2)   # 3     - floor division, discards the fraction
+print(7 % 2)    # 1     - remainder (modulo)
+print(2 ** 10)  # 1024  - exponent
+```
+
+`%` shows up constantly in scripting: `n % 2 == 0` tests even numbers, and `i % 10 == 0` triggers something every tenth iteration.
+
+## Booleans and Truthiness
+
+`True` and `False` are the only two booleans. Comparisons produce them: `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, plus membership (`in`) and identity (`is`):
+
+```python
+port = 22
+print(port == 22)              # True
+print("ssh" in ["ssh", "http"])  # True
+```
+
+Every value also has an implicit truth value. **Falsy** values: `False`, `0`, `0.0`, `""`, `[]`, `{}`, `set()`, `None`. Everything else is truthy. Conditions use this directly:
+
+```python
+findings = []
+if findings:
+    print("issues found")
 else:
-    print(f"Twice the count is {count * 2}")
+    print("clean")   # empty list is falsy, so this prints
+```
+
+```text
+clean
 ```

+## None and Type Conversion
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.

+`None` is the single "no value" object. Functions that return nothing actually return `None`. Test for it with `is`, never `==`:
+
+```python

+if result is None:
+    print("nothing yet")
+```
+
+Converting between types is explicit:
+
+```python
+int("443")        # 443
+int("443", 10)    # same, base 10; base 16 parses hex strings
+float("3.5")      # 3.5
+str(443)          # '443'
+bool("")          # False
+```
+
+`int("12.5")` raises `ValueError` (it is not an integer literal); go through `float` first if the input might have a decimal point. `int("0x1F", 16)` and `int("1F", 16)` both give 31 - useful when parsing hex from dumps and protocol output.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Comparing floats with `==` and chasing ghosts; use `math.isclose`.
+- Testing `if x == None` instead of `if x is None`. It often works, but `==` can be overridden by classes while `is` checks identity.
+- Expecting `/` to behave like integer division; in Python 3 it always yields a float, so use `//` when you want an integer quotient.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `int` never overflows; `float` has rounding error, so compare with `math.isclose`.
+- `/` always divides to a float; `//` floors, `%` gives the remainder, `**` raises to a power.
+- Empty containers, zero, `""`, and `None` are falsy; test `None` with `is`, and convert types explicitly with `int()`, `float()`, `str()`.

 ## Introduction
 
-Strings are immutable Unicode text sequences. Useful methods include `strip`, `lower`, `upper`, `replace`, `split`, `join`, `startswith`, and `endswith`. Use f-strings for readable interpolation.
+Text is the most common data a script touches: log lines, URLs, usernames, file contents, protocol banners. Python strings are **immutable sequences of Unicode characters**. Immutable means every "modification" method returns a new string; the original never changes:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+host = "Example.COM"
+print(host.lower())   # example.com
+print(host)           # Example.COM - unchanged
+```

+## Indexing and Slicing
+
+Positions count from 0; negative indexes count from the end. Slices are `start:stop:step`, with `stop` excluded:
+
+```python
+s = "GET /admin HTTP/1.1"
+print(s[0])        # G
+print(s[-7:])      # HTTP/1.1
+print(s[4:11])     # /admin
+print(s[::2])      # GTadm TP1.
+print(s[::-1])     # 1.1/PTTH nimda/ TEG - reversed
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Slicing never raises `IndexError` for out-of-range bounds; it just returns what overlaps. That makes `line[:80]` a safe way to truncate anything.
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## The Methods You Will Use Daily
 
 ```python

-name = raw_name.strip().lower()

-## Practical Exercise
+The `split`/`join` pair is the workhorse of log parsing: split a line into fields, pick the ones you want, join results back for output. For fixed-format or messy text, regular expressions (the `re` module) take over; there is a dedicated treatment in the log-parser project in module 17.

+## f-Strings: Formatting Done Right
+
+Prefixed with `f`, a string interpolates any expression inside braces:
 
-Normalize a username, split a simple comma-separated record, and join words with spaces. For real CSV with quoted commas, use the `csv` module.
+```python
+user = "alice"
+fails = 5
+print(f"{user} failed {fails} times ({fails / 10:.1%})")

+```
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.

+```text
+alice failed 5 times (50.0%)
+host          port
+web-1           443
+```
+
+Format specifiers after `:` control width, alignment (`<` left, `>` right, `^` center), and precision (`.2f` two decimals, `.1%` percent). Aligned columns make scan output and reports readable.
+
+## Strings and Bytes
+
+Networks and files often deal in **bytes**, not text. A `bytes` literal looks like `b"GET"`. Convert with `.encode()` (str to bytes) and `.decode()` (bytes to str); UTF-8 is the default and the right choice almost always:
+
+```python
+data = "hello".encode()       # b'hello'
+text = data.decode()          # 'hello'
+payload = "user=admin".encode("utf-8")
+```
+
+Mixing them without converting raises `TypeError`. When a socket hands you bytes, decode once at the boundary and work in text inside the program.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Forgetting strings are immutable: calling `line.strip()` and then using `line` again unchanged. Capture the return value: `line = line.strip()`.
+- Splitting on a single space when data has runs of whitespace. Bare `.split()` (no argument) splits on any whitespace run and drops empties, which is usually what you want for logs.
+- Building big strings with `+` in a loop. Collect pieces in a list and `"".join(pieces)` once; it is faster and cleaner.

+- f-strings format and align output; encode/decode at the boundary between text and bytes.

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Use arithmetic, comparison, membership, and identity operators correctly.
+- Combine conditions with `and`, `or`, `not` and predict short-circuit behavior.
+- Read operator precedence well enough to write unambiguous expressions.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Python provides arithmetic (`+ - * / // % **`), comparison (`== != < <= > >=`), logical (`and or not`), assignment (`+=`), membership (`in`), and identity (`is`) operators. Parentheses clarify precedence. `==` compares values; `is` checks object identity. Use `is None` for a None check.
+An *expression* is anything Python can evaluate to a value: `2 + 3`, `port == 22`, `"error" in line`. Operators are the verbs. Most behave as you expect from math and other languages; this lesson focuses on the parts Python does differently and the combinations real scripts lean on.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Arithmetic and Assignment
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Beyond `+ - * / // % **` (previous lesson), the augmented forms update in place:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+total = 0
+total += 5     # same as total = total + 5
+retries = 3
+retries -= 1
+mask = 0b1100
+mask |= 0b0011 # bitwise OR assignment -> 0b1111
+```
+
+Bitwise operators (`&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, `<<`, `>>`) matter in security work: subnet masks, file permission bits, flags packed into integers, and simple obfuscation all use them.
+
+```python
+0b1100 & 0b1010   # 0b1000  (8) - bits set in both
+0b1100 | 0b1010   # 0b1110  (14) - bits set in either
+0b1100 ^ 0b1010   # 0b0110  (6) - bits set in exactly one
+1 << 4            # 16 - shift left multiplies by powers of two
+```
+
+## Comparison, Membership, Identity
+
+```python
+code = 404
+200 <= code < 500     # True - chained comparison, reads like math
+"admin" in ["root", "admin"]   # True - membership
+"GET" not in "POST / HTTP"     # True
+a = None
+a is None             # True - identity, the correct None test
+```
+
+Two traps live here:
+
+1. `==` compares *values*; `is` compares *identity* (same object). Use `is` only for singletons: `None`, `True`, `False`.
+2. Chained comparisons are evaluated pairwise: `200 <= code < 500` means `(200 <= code) and (code < 500)`.
+
+## Boolean Operators and Short-Circuiting
+
+`and` returns the first falsy operand or the last value; `or` returns the first truthy operand or the last value. Evaluation stops as soon as the result is known - this is **short-circuiting**, and it has practical uses:
 
 ```python
-port = 443
-allowed = port == 80 or port == 443
-print(allowed)       # True
-print(29 // 5, 29 % 5)  # 5 4
-print(None is None)  # True
+# Only calls expensive_check() if cached is empty:
+if not cached and expensive_check():
+    ...
+
+# Default value pattern:
+name = supplied_name or "anonymous"
+
+# Guard pattern: len(parts) is only evaluated when parts is non-empty
+if parts and len(parts) > 2:
+    ...
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Without short-circuiting, `len(parts) > 2` would still run on an empty list. With it, the guard and the use sit safely in one expression. `not` flips truthiness and always returns a real boolean.
+
+## Precedence: When in Doubt, Parenthesize
+
+The rough order, highest first: `**` → unary `-`, `not` → `* / // %` → `+ -` → comparisons, `in`, `is` → `and` → `or`. So:
 
-Check whether a port is 80 or 443; calculate quotient and remainder for 29 divided by 5; compare two separate equal lists using `==` and `is`.
+```python
+2 + 3 * 4          # 14, not 20
+not 1 in [1, 2]    # False: parsed as not (1 in [1, 2])
+True or False and False  # True: 'and' binds tighter than 'or'
+```
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Nobody memorizes the whole table. Write `a and (b or c)` when grouping matters; the parentheses cost nothing and save the next reader a bug.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Writing `if port == 22 or 80:` - this is always truthy because `80` is truthy. Say `if port in (22, 80):`.
+- Using `is` to compare strings or numbers. CPython interns some small values so it *appears* to work, then fails on other values. Values: `==`. Identity: `is`.
+- Forgetting that `and`/`or` return operands, not booleans: `"" or 0` is `0`, not `False`. Wrap in `bool()` if you need a strict boolean.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Augmented assignment (`+=`, `|=`) updates variables concisely; bitwise operators unpack flags and masks.
+- `==` for values, `is` for `None`/singletons, `in` for membership; chained comparisons read like math.
+- `and`/`or` short-circuit, enabling guard and default-value patterns; parenthesize mixed boolean logic.
+

+

+

+
+```python

+    print("log enabled")

+    print("block enabled")

+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+

+    print("known service port")
+```

+Membership on a tuple is clearer and cannot fall into the `or 80` trap.
+</details>

+**Exercise 3.** Without running it, predict `True or False and False` and `0 or "" or "fallback"`, then verify in the REPL.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+```text
+True
+'fallback'
+```
+
+`and` binds tighter, so the first is `True or (False and False)`. In the second, both `0` and `""` are falsy, so `or` continues to `"fallback"`, the first truthy operand.
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**What is the difference between `==` and `is`?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`==` compares values (what `__eq__` defines); `is` compares object identity. Use `is` for `None`, `True`, `False`; use `==` for everything else.
+</details>
+
+**Why does `if port == 22 or 80:` always pass?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+It parses as `(port == 22) or (80)`, and `80` is truthy, so the whole expression is always truthy. The fix is `if port in (22, 80):`.
+</details>
+
+**What does `"0x10" and 16` evaluate to?**
 
-## Further Practice
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-Modify the example, explain each line in your own words, and link the exercise to the next module.
+`16`. `and` returns the last operand when both are truthy; `"0x10"` is a non-empty string, hence truthy.
+</details>

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Structure decisions with `if` / `elif` / `else` and understand evaluation order.
+- Choose between nested conditions, chained comparisons, and conditional expressions.
+- Handle edge cases deliberately instead of hoping inputs are well-behaved.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Conditions choose which block executes. Python checks branches from top to bottom and runs the first true branch. Nested conditions are useful when a decision depends on an earlier decision; conditional expressions suit short choices.
-
-## Core Concepts
-
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
-
-## Syntax and Example
+A conditional runs the **first** branch whose test is truthy, then skips the rest. Order is therefore part of the logic: put specific cases before general ones.
 
 ```python
 code = 404
+
 if 200 <= code < 300:
     label = "success"
 elif 300 <= code < 400:
     label = "redirect"
 elif 400 <= code < 500:
     label = "client error"
+elif 500 <= code < 600:
+    label = "server error"
 else:
-    label = "other"
-print(label)
+    label = "not an HTTP status"
+
+print(f"{code}: {label}")
+```
+
+```text
+404: client error
+```
+
+The `else` branch is the catch-all. Leaving it out means "do nothing if nothing matched," which is fine when that is truly the intent - and a silent bug when you actually forgot a case.
+
+## Nested Conditions and Early Structure
+
+Conditions can nest, and sometimes a decision genuinely depends on an earlier one:
+
+```python
+if user:
+    if user.is_admin:
+        show_admin_panel()
+    else:
+        show_dashboard()
+else:
+    show_login()
+```
+
+Two structural habits keep nesting shallow:
+
+1. **Combine with boolean operators** when the branches share a body:
+   `if user and user.is_admin:` replaces one level.
+2. **Guard clauses**: handle the failing/edge case first and exit (with `return` in a function, module 07), so the main path stays at low indentation. Deep pyramids of `if` inside `if` inside `if` are where bugs hide.
+
+## Conditional Expressions
+
+For picking between two values, the inline form is clearer than a four-line `if`:
+
+```python
+status = "open" if result == 0 else "closed"
+severity = "high" if fails > 10 else "normal"
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Use it for simple either/or values only. If the condition or the branches need explanation, a normal `if` block reads better.
 
-Read a status code and classify 2xx, 3xx, 4xx, and 5xx responses. Add a fallback for values outside those ranges.
+## Defensive Branching
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Real input is hostile or sloppy. Branch on *validated* data:
+
+```python
+raw = input("Port: ")
+if not raw.isdigit():
+    print("port must be numeric")
+else:
+    port = int(raw)
+    if 1 <= port <= 65535:
+        print(f"checking port {port}")
+    else:
+        print("port out of range")
+```
+
+Checking `isdigit()` before `int()` is a common and readable validation pattern for interactive scripts. (For non-interactive tools, `try`/`except ValueError` around `int()` is equally good; module 11 compares the two styles.)
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Ordering ranges wrong: testing `code < 500` before `code < 400` makes the 4xx branch unreachable. Specific conditions first.
+- Repeating `or` with implicit subjects: `if code == 404 or 403:` is always truthy (see module 04). Use `in (404, 403)`.
+- Using nested `if`s three levels deep when a guard clause or a combined condition would flatten the logic.
+- Forgetting that an empty `elif` chain without `else` silently does nothing on unexpected input.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `if`/`elif`/`else` evaluates top to bottom and runs only the first match; order branches from specific to general.
+- Flatten with combined conditions and guard clauses; keep nesting shallow.
+- Use conditional expressions for simple either/or values, and validate input before branching on it.
+

+
+**Why does the 5xx check come after the 4xx check in the status classifier?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Branches run in order, and each `elif` only sees values all previous tests rejected. Ordering ascending ranges (2xx, 3xx, 4xx, 5xx) keeps each condition simple and every branch reachable.
+</details>
+
+**What is a guard clause?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+An early branch that handles the invalid or trivial case and exits (or continues), so the main logic is not wrapped in extra indentation.
+</details>
+

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+```python
+if ok:
+    x = "yes"
+else:
+    x = "no"
+```
+</details>

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Iterate over sequences, files, and enumerations with `for`.
+- Use `range`, `enumerate`, and `zip` to control what you loop over.

 ## Introduction
 
-A `for` loop iterates over items in an iterable such as a list, string, or `range`. `range(stop)` excludes the stop value; `range(start, stop, step)` controls the sequence. Nested loops run the inner loop for each outer iteration.
+Python's `for` is a **for-each** loop: it visits each item of an iterable in order. There is no C-style `for (i=0; i<n; i++)` - when you need numbers, you iterate over a `range`:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+for port in (22, 80, 443):
+    print("checking", port)
+
+for i in range(3):        # 0, 1, 2
+    print("attempt", i)
+
+for i in range(10, 0, -2):  # 10, 8, 6, 4, 2
+    print(i)
+```
+
+```text
+checking 22
+checking 80
+checking 443
+attempt 0
+attempt 1
+attempt 2
+10
+8
+6
+4
+2
+```
+
+`range(start, stop, step)` excludes `stop` and is lazy: `range(10**9)` costs no memory because values are produced on demand.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## Looping with Index and in Parallel
+
+Need the position as well as the value? Use `enumerate`, not a manual counter:
+
+```python
+hosts = ["web-1", "db-1", "vpn-gw"]
+for i, host in enumerate(hosts, start=1):
+    print(f"{i}. {host}")
+```
+
+```text
+1. web-1
+2. db-1
+3. vpn-gw
+```
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Loop over two lists in lockstep with `zip`:
 
 ```python
-ports = [22, 80, 443, 8080]
-for port in ports:
-    if port <= 1024:
-        print(port)
+ports = [22, 80, 443]
+services = ["ssh", "http", "https"]
+for port, service in zip(ports, services):
+    print(f"{service} runs on {port}")
+```
+
+`zip` stops at the shorter input; `zip(strict=True)` raises instead if lengths differ.
+
+## Iterating Dictionaries and Files
+
+Dictionaries iterate keys by default; `.items()` gives pairs. Files iterate line by line without loading everything into memory:
 
-for number in range(1, 6):
-    print(number)
+```python
+scores = {"alice": 9, "bob": 4}
+for name, score in scores.items():
+    print(name, score)
+
+with open("app.log") as f:
+    for line_number, line in enumerate(f, start=1):
+        if "error" in line.lower():
+            print(line_number, line.strip())
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Line-by-line file iteration is the pattern behind every log analyzer in this course: it handles a 10 GB log in constant memory.
 
-Filter a list to ports in 1..1024, count `.log` filenames, and print numbers from 10 down to 1.
+## Comprehensions
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+When a loop exists only to build a list, a comprehension says it in one expression:
+
+```python
+codes = [200, 301, 404, 500, 204]
+errors = [c for c in codes if c >= 400]          # [404, 500]
+labels = {c: ("error" if c >= 400 else "ok") for c in codes}
+squares = [n * n for n in range(5)]               # [0, 1, 4, 9, 16]
+```
+
+Shape: `[expression for item in iterable if condition]`. Keep comprehensions to one idea; if the expression needs a second line or a nested condition tree, a plain loop is clearer.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Modifying a list while iterating over it (removing matches mid-loop skips elements). Build a new list with a comprehension instead.
+- Writing `for i in range(len(hosts)): hosts[i]` when `for host in hosts:` or `enumerate` is available.
+- Nesting comprehensions three deep to look clever. Nobody, including future you, will debug it happily.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `for` iterates items directly; `range(start, stop, step)` supplies number sequences and is lazy.
+- `enumerate` adds positions, `zip` pairs sequences, files iterate line by line.
+- Comprehensions turn "filter/transform into a new collection" loops into one readable expression.

+# while Loops, break, and continue
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Loop on a condition with `while` and guarantee termination.
+- Control iteration with `break`, `continue`, and the loop `else`.
+- Recognize and avoid infinite loops and off-by-one errors.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-A `while` loop repeats while its condition is true. `break` exits the nearest loop; `continue` skips to its next iteration; `pass` does nothing. Ensure the condition changes or the loop has an exit to avoid accidental infinite loops.
+`for` answers "for each item"; `while` answers "as long as this holds." It re-checks its condition before every pass:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+retries = 3
+while retries > 0:
+    print("trying...")
+    retries -= 1
+print("giving up")
+```
+
+```text
+trying...
+trying...
+trying...
+giving up
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Two patterns dominate real scripts:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+1. **Retry with a budget** (above): count down attempts until success or exhaustion.
+2. **Read until done**: loop until input, a socket, or a stream signals the end.
 
 ```python
-attempts = 0
-while attempts < 3:
-    print(f"Attempt {attempts + 1}")
-    attempts += 1
+while True:
+    line = input("cmd> ")
+    if line == "quit":
+        break
+    print("echo:", line)
+```
 
-for value in ["a", "", "b"]:
-    if not value:
-        continue
-    print(value)
+`while True` plus a `break` on a clear exit condition is idiomatic for interactive loops - but only when the exit is obvious and reachable.
+
+## break, continue, and the Loop else
+
+- `break` leaves the loop immediately.
+- `continue` skips to the next iteration.
+- A loop's `else:` block runs **only if the loop finished without `break`**. This is the least-known Python feature and perfect for search loops:
+
+```python
+ports = [21, 22, 80, 443]
+target = 23
+for port in ports:
+    if port == target:
+        print("found:", port)
+        break
+else:
+    print(target, "not in the list")
+```
+
+```text
+23 not in the list
+```
+
+Without the loop-`else`, you would need a `found = False` flag. The same structure works on `while`.
+
+A practical `continue` example - skip noise lines when parsing:
+
+```python
+for line in open("app.log"):
+    line = line.strip()
+    if not line or line.startswith("#"):
+        continue          # skip blanks and comments
+    process(line)
+```
+
+## Termination and Defensive Loops
+
+Every `while` needs an argument for why it must end. Ask: *what changes each iteration, and does the condition eventually become false?*
+
+```python
+# BUG: countdown never changes if n starts negative... and never changes at all here
+n = 5
+while n != 0:
+    print(n)
+    n -= 1
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+This one terminates for positive `n`, but `while n != 0` with `n -= 1` loops forever if `n` starts at -1 (it skips past 0). The robust form is `while n > 0:`. Prefer inequalities over exact-equality exit tests; they tolerate unexpected starting values.
 
-Ask repeatedly until a positive integer is entered. Search a list and stop at the first match. Identify the state change that guarantees termination.
+For network and retry code, always bound the loop: a maximum attempt count or a deadline (`time.monotonic()`), plus `sleep` between tries so you do not hammer the target:
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+import time
+
+deadline = time.monotonic() + 10   # give up after 10 seconds
+while time.monotonic() < deadline:
+    if try_connect():
+        break
+    time.sleep(0.5)
+else:
+    print("host unreachable")
+```
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- `while True` with no reachable `break` - the script hangs forever and has to be killed (Ctrl-C).
+- Updating the loop variable on only some paths, so certain inputs loop forever.
+- Using `!=` as the exit test where `<`/`>` would be safe against skipped values.
+- Polling in a tight loop with no sleep, burning CPU and tripping rate limits.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `while` repeats while its condition holds; `while True` + `break` suits read-until-done loops.
+- `break` exits, `continue` skips ahead, and loop `else` runs only when no `break` happened - ideal for search loops.
+- Guarantee termination: something must move toward the exit each pass, inequalities beat equality tests, and external waits need a deadline.
+

+

+## Check Yourself
+
+**When does a loop's `else` block execute?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+When the loop condition becomes false (or iteration exhausts) without any `break` having run. A `break` skips the `else`.
+</details>
+
+**Why is `while n > 0` safer than `while n != 0` for a decrementing counter?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+If `n` ever starts negative or steps past zero, `!=` never becomes false and the loop runs forever. `>` terminates for any non-positive value.
+</details>
+
+**What does `continue` do inside a file-parsing loop?**
 
-## Further Practice

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.

 ## Introduction
 
-Functions package reusable tasks. Define with `def`; parameters receive inputs and `return` sends a result to the caller. `print()` displays output and is not equivalent to returning a value. Functions without an explicit return produce `None`.
+A function packages a named, reusable piece of logic. Define with `def`, call with parentheses:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+def classify_port(port):
+    if port == 22:
+        return "ssh"
+    if port == 80:
+        return "http"
+    if port == 443:
+        return "https"
+    return "unknown"
+
+print(classify_port(22))    # ssh
+print(classify_port(9000))  # unknown
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+`return` ends the function immediately and hands a value back; code after it in the same block never runs. A function with no `return` (or a bare `return`) gives back `None`:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+def log(msg):
+    print("LOG:", msg)
+
+result = log("scan started")
+print(result)   # None
+```
+
+## Parameters, Defaults, and Keyword Calls
 
 ```python
-def is_valid_port(port):
-    return isinstance(port, int) and not isinstance(port, bool) and 1 <= port <= 65535
+def scan(host, ports=(80, 443), timeout=1.5):
+    for port in ports:
+        print(f"{host}:{port} (timeout {timeout}s)")
+
+scan("web-1")                        # uses defaults
+scan("web-1", (22, 80))              # positional
+scan("web-1", timeout=3)             # keyword: readable, order-free
+```
+
+Rules that matter:
+
+- Parameters with defaults must come after those without.
+- Keyword arguments (`timeout=3`) document the call site; use them once a function takes more than two parameters.
+- **Never use a mutable default.** `def f(items=[])` creates one list shared by every call:
+
+```python
+def bad(items=[]):
+    items.append(1)
+    return items
+
+print(bad())  # [1]
+print(bad())  # [1, 1] - same list remembered!
 
-print(is_valid_port(443))
+def good(items=None):
+    if items is None:
+        items = []
+    items.append(1)
+    return items
+
+print(good())  # [1]
+print(good())  # [1] - fresh list each time
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+## Docstrings and Small Functions

+The first string inside a function is its docstring, surfaced by `help()`:
 
-Write functions for username normalization, failed-event counting, and min/max calculation. Keep each function focused on one responsibility.

-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Functions should do one thing and fit on a screen. When a script grows, split it: one function to read input, one to transform, one to output. Each becomes testable in isolation and the main flow reads like a table of contents:
+
+```python
+def main():
+    lines = read_lines("app.log")
+    errors = find_errors(lines)
+    report(errors)
+
+main()
+```
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Printing inside a function that should return a value, so callers cannot reuse the result. Compute and `return`; let the caller print.
+- The mutable-default trap (`def f(x=[])`).
+- Calling `my_func` without parentheses and wondering why nothing happens - that references the function object; the call is `my_func()`.
+- Giant 100-line functions that mix parsing, logic, and output.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `def` defines, `return` sends back exactly one object (or `None`); functions are values themselves.
+- Default and keyword arguments make calls flexible; mutable defaults are a shared-state bug - use `None` and build inside.
+- Docstrings document; small single-purpose functions keep scripts testable.

+    return items

+print(track())  # ['x', 'x']  <- bug: remembered

+def track_fixed(items=None):
+    if items is None:
+        items = []
+    items.append("x")
+    return items

+print(track_fixed())  # ['x']   <- correct
+```

+**Why is `def f(items=[])` dangerous?**
+
+<details>

+The default list is created once at definition time and shared by every call, so mutations accumulate across calls. Use `items=None` and create the list inside.

+# Arguments, Return Values, and Scope
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.

 ## Introduction
 
-Positional arguments match by position; keyword arguments by name; defaults apply when omitted. `*args` collects extra positional arguments in a tuple and `**kwargs` collects keyword arguments in a dictionary. Local names belong to a function scope. `global` and `nonlocal` rebind outer names but should be used sparingly. Docstrings document functions; lambdas are short anonymous functions.
+This lesson covers the remaining mechanics of functions: flexible argument lists, multiple return values, and where a name "lives." These show up constantly when you read library code or write utilities other functions build on.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## *args and **kwargs
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Sometimes a function cannot know how many values it will receive:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+def summarize(*numbers):
+    """Accept any count of positional values."""
+    return sum(numbers), max(numbers), min(numbers)
+
+total, high, low = summarize(3, 9, 4, 9, 1)
+print(total, high, low)   # 26 9 1
+```
+
+`*numbers` gathers extra positional arguments into a tuple. `**options` gathers extra keyword arguments into a dict:
+
+```python
+def connect(host, **options):
+    print("host:", host)
+    for key, value in options.items():
+        print(f"  {key} = {value}")
+
+connect("web-1", port=443, tls=True, timeout=2)
+```
+
+```text
+host: web-1
+  port = 443
+  tls = True
+  timeout = 2
+```
+
+The names `args`/`kwargs` are convention; the stars do the work. You also use the stars at the call site to *spread* a collection into arguments: `f(*[1, 2, 3])` calls `f(1, 2, 3)`.
+
+## Multiple Return Values
+
+`return a, b, c` actually returns one tuple; callers unpack it:
 
 ```python
-def report(*items, severity="info", **metadata):
-    """Display a compact event summary."""
-    print(items, severity, metadata)
+def parse_line(line):
+    ip, _, status = line.partition(" ")
+    return ip, status          # one tuple of two
 
-report("login", "file", severity="low", source="lab")
+ip, status = parse_line("10.0.0.5 200")
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Unpacking fails loudly on a count mismatch, which is good - it means structure changes are caught early. If callers regularly ignore one value, name it `_`.
 
-Write a function accepting any number of numeric positional arguments and returning their sum. Add a docstring. Explain why mutable default arguments such as `items=[]` can be surprising.
+## Scope: Where Names Live
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Names defined inside a function are **local** to it and vanish when it returns. Names at module top level are **global**. Reading follows the LEGB rule: Local, Enclosing, Global, Built-in - Python checks in that order:
+
+```python
+attempts = 0          # global
+
+def try_login():
+    attempts = 1      # creates a NEW local; the global is untouched
+    print("inside:", attempts)
+
+try_login()           # inside: 1
+print("outside:", attempts)   # outside: 0
+```
+
+Assignment inside a function creates a local by default. To deliberately modify a global, declare `global attempts` first - but treat that as a design smell: passing values in as parameters and returning results keeps functions predictable and testable.
+
+Closures are the useful side of scope: an inner function remembers the names from the function that created it.
+
+```python
+def make_checker(limit):
+    def check(value):
+        return value <= limit
+    return check
+
+under_1024 = make_checker(1024)
+print(under_1024(443))    # True
+print(under_1024(8080))   # False
+```
+
+`check` still sees `limit` after `make_checker` returned - that captured state is a closure, and it is how small configurable checkers get built without classes.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Assuming assignment inside a function updates the global; it shadows it instead. Use parameters and return values.
+- `UnboundLocalError` from reading a name and assigning it later in the same function - Python marks it local for the whole function, so the earlier read fails.
+- Overusing `*args/**kwargs` to dodge designing a real signature; explicit parameters document themselves.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `*args` and `**kwargs` collect variable arguments; stars also spread collections at call sites.
+- Multiple returns are tuple packing/unpacking; name ignored values `_`.
+- Assignment creates locals; read order is Local, Enclosing, Global, Built-in. Prefer parameters over `global`.
+

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Create, index, slice, and mutate lists without aliasing surprises.
+- Use the core list methods and know their costs.
