 ## Introduction
 
-Lists are ordered and mutable. Use indexes (starting at zero), negative indexes, and slices. Methods include `append`, `extend`, `insert`, `remove`, `pop`, and `sort`. List comprehensions create transformed or filtered lists. Avoid removing items from a list while iterating over it.
+A list is an ordered, mutable sequence - the default container in Python. It holds anything, keeps insertion order, and allows duplicates:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+ports = [22, 80, 443, 8080]
+ports.append(9000)        # add at end -> [22, 80, 443, 8080, 9000]
+ports.insert(1, 21)       # insert at index -> [22, 21, 80, 443, 8080, 9000]
+ports.remove(21)          # remove first matching VALUE
+last = ports.pop()        # remove and return the last item -> 9000
+ports.sort()              # in place -> [22, 80, 443, 8080]
+print(len(ports), 443 in ports)   # 4 True
+```
+
+Indexing and slicing work exactly like strings (module 03): `ports[0]`, `ports[-1]`, `ports[1:3]`, `ports[::-1]`.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## Methods, Sorted vs sort, and Copies
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Two operations have a "mutate in place" version and a "return a new one" version; mixing them up is a classic bug:
 
 ```python
-names = ["ana", "bo", "cy"]
-upper = [name.upper() for name in names]
-print(names[0], names[-1], upper)
+ports = [8080, 22, 443]
+ports.sort()          # mutates ports itself, returns None
+ordered = sorted(ports)   # returns a NEW sorted list, ports untouched
+backwards = sorted(ports, reverse=True)
+by_length = sorted(["bbb", "a", "cc"], key=len)   # ['a', 'cc', 'bbb']
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Assigning `ports = ports.sort()` wipes the list to `None` - `sort()` returns nothing. Similarly `list(reversed(x))` vs `x.reverse()`.
+
+Other daily tools:
+
+```python
+ports.index(443)       # 2 - position of first match (ValueError if absent)
+ports.count(22)        # how many times a value appears
+", ".join(map(str, ports))   # '22, 443, 8080' for display
+ports.clear()          # empty it
+copy = ports[:]        # shallow copy - aliasing rules from module 03 apply
+```
+
+## Cost and Choosing Structures
+
+Appending and popping at the end are O(1) - effectively instant. Inserting or deleting at the front is O(n) because everything shifts; if a script pops from the front in a hot loop, use `collections.deque`, which is O(1) at both ends. Membership tests (`x in big_list`) scan linearly; for frequent membership checks on large collections, a `set` is the right tool (next lessons).
 
-Filter `.log` filenames, remove duplicates while preserving order, and create a 2-by-2 nested list.
+```python
+from collections import deque
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+queue = deque(["task-1", "task-2"])
+queue.append("task-3")     # right end
+print(queue.popleft())     # task-1 - fast from the left
+```
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- `my_list = my_list.sort()` - destroys the list (`sort` returns `None`).
+- Removing items while iterating (module 06); build a filtered list instead.
+- Using a list for thousands of `in` checks per second; that is what sets are for.
+- `*` replication of nested lists: `[[0] * 3] * 3` creates three references to the same inner row. Use a comprehension: `[[0] * 3 for _ in range(3)]`.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Lists are ordered and mutable; learn `append`, `insert`, `remove`, `pop`, `index`, `count`.
+- `sort()`/`reverse()` mutate in place and return `None`; `sorted()`/`reversed()` return new sequences.
+- End operations are O(1); front operations and membership scans are O(n) - reach for `deque` or `set` when that matters.

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Store and retrieve key-value data with dicts, and handle missing keys safely.
+- Count and group data - the two jobs dicts do better than anything else.
+- Know what can be a key and how dict views and iteration behave.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Dictionaries map unique hashable keys to values. Use `d[key]` for required keys and `d.get(key, default)` for optional keys. Iterate with `.items()`. Dictionaries can contain nested dictionaries and support comprehensions.
+A dictionary maps **keys to values** - the structure behind counts, lookups, configuration, and JSON. Lookup by key is O(1) on average:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+services = {22: "ssh", 80: "http", 443: "https"}
+print(services[443])            # https
+services[53] = "dns"            # insert or overwrite
+del services[80]                # remove
+print(22 in services)           # True - tests KEYS
+```
+
+Reading a missing key raises `KeyError`. The safe forms:
+
+```python
+services.get(25)                # None - no exception
+services.get(25, "unknown")     # 'unknown' - custom default
+name = services.setdefault(3306, "mysql")   # insert if missing, then return
+```
+
+Keys must be **hashable**: strings, numbers, tuples (of hashables) work; lists and dicts do not.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## Counting and Grouping
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Counting occurrences is the single most common dict pattern in log analysis:
 
 ```python
 counts = {}
-for severity in ["info", "warning", "info"]:
-    counts[severity] = counts.get(severity, 0) + 1
-print(counts)
+for line in open("app.log"):
+    if "Failed password" in line:
+        user = line.rsplit(" for ", 1)[-1].strip()
+        counts[user] = counts.get(user, 0) + 1
+print(counts)   # {'admin': 12, 'root': 31, 'alice': 2}
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Two refinements from the standard library:
 
-Create a log-event dictionary. Count severity values and build a dictionary containing only severities with counts greater than one.
+```python
+from collections import Counter, defaultdict
+
+counts = Counter(usernames)          # one line; counts.most_common(3) ranks
+by_status = defaultdict(list)        # missing key auto-creates an empty list
+by_status[code].append(url)
+```
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Grouping - collecting items under a category - is the same pattern with lists as values, and `defaultdict(list)` removes the `setdefault` ceremony.
+
+## Iteration and Views
+
+```python
+for port in services:                # keys (insertion order, guaranteed)
+    ...
+for port, name in services.items():  # pairs
+    ...
+list(services.keys()); list(services.values())
+```
+
+Since Python 3.7, dicts preserve insertion order. Changing a dict's size *while iterating it* raises `RuntimeError`; iterate over `list(services)` when deleting entries. `keys()`, `values()`, and `items()` return live views that reflect later changes.
+
+Merging and building:
+
+```python
+defaults = {"timeout": 3, "retries": 2}
+config = {**defaults, "retries": 5}   # later wins -> {'timeout': 3, 'retries': 5}
+squares = {n: n * n for n in range(4)}  # dict comprehension
+```
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- `services[25]` on a key that might not exist; use `.get()` when absence is normal.
+- Forgetting `in` tests keys, not values.
+- Using a list or dict as a key (`TypeError: unhashable type`) - use a tuple for compound keys.
+- Mutating a dict during iteration over the dict itself.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Dicts map hashable keys to values with O(1) lookup; `.get()` and `setdefault()` handle missing keys gracefully.
+- `counts[k] = counts.get(k, 0) + 1` counts; `defaultdict(list)` groups; `Counter` does both faster.
+- Iteration yields keys in insertion order; `.items()` yields pairs; never resize a dict mid-loop.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Count word frequencies in `"the cat sat on the mat the cat ran"` and print the two most common words with their counts.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+from collections import Counter
+
+words = "the cat sat on the mat the cat ran".split()
+counts = Counter(words)
+print(counts.most_common(2))
+```
+
+```text
+[('the', 3), ('cat', 2)]
+```
+
+Without Counter: `counts[w] = counts.get(w, 0) + 1` in a loop.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Given log entries like `"200 /index"`, `"404 /admin"`, `"200 /about"`, group paths by status code into a dict of lists.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+from collections import defaultdict
+
+entries = ["200 /index", "404 /admin", "200 /about"]
+by_status = defaultdict(list)
+for entry in entries:
+    code, path = entry.split()
+    by_status[code].append(path)
+print(dict(by_status))
+```
+
+```text
+{'200': ['/index', '/about'], '404': ['/admin']}
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Merge `{"a": 1, "b": 2}` and `{"b": 99, "c": 3}` so the second dict wins on conflicts, without mutating either original.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+first = {"a": 1, "b": 2}
+second = {"b": 99, "c": 3}
+merged = {**first, **second}
+print(merged)   # {'a': 1, 'b': 99, 'c': 3}
+print(first)    # {'a': 1, 'b': 2} - untouched
+```
+</details>
+

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Indexing raises `KeyError`; `.get()` returns the default (or `None` if none is given) without raising.
+</details>
+
+**Which types can serve as dict keys?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Hashable (immutable) types: strings, numbers, tuples containing hashables. Lists, dicts, and sets cannot.
+</details>

 ## Learning Objectives

 ## Introduction
 
-Tuples are ordered and immutable; unpack them into names. A one-item tuple needs a comma. Sets contain unique hashable values and support union (`|`), intersection (`&`), difference (`-`), and membership tests. An empty set is `set()`, not `{}`.
+Tuples and sets round out the built-in containers. A **tuple** is an ordered, immutable sequence; a **set** is an unordered collection of unique, hashable items with fast membership tests.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Tuples: Fixed Records
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```python
+finding = ("10.0.0.5", 22, "ssh")
+host, port, service = finding     # unpacking
+print(port)                       # 22
+pair = 1, 2                       # parentheses optional
+single = (1,)                     # trailing comma REQUIRED for one item
+```
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Tuples communicate "this shape is fixed": a coordinate, a database row, a function returning host-plus-port. Immutability means no `append` or item assignment - but it also makes tuples hashable, so they can be dict keys and set members:
 
 ```python
-observed = {"192.0.2.1", "192.0.2.2"}
-known = {"192.0.2.2", "192.0.2.3"}
-print(observed & known)
+connections = {("10.0.0.5", 22), ("10.0.0.5", 443), ("10.0.0.9", 22)}
+counts = {("10.0.0.5", 22): 14}   # compound key
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+`namedtuple` (from `collections`) gives tuple fields names when plain indexes get cryptic:
+
+```python
+from collections import namedtuple
+Finding = namedtuple("Finding", ["host", "port", "service"])
+f = Finding("10.0.0.5", 22, "ssh")
+print(f.service)   # ssh - self-documenting
+```
 
-Unpack a username/role tuple. Find unique IPs and the intersection of two IP sets. Explain when a list, dictionary, tuple, or set is the clearest choice.
+## Sets: Uniqueness and Membership
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+open_ports = {22, 80, 443, 80}
+print(open_ports)        # {80, 22, 443} - duplicates dropped
+open_ports.add(8080)
+open_ports.discard(21)   # remove if present, no error if absent
+print(443 in open_ports) # True - O(1) even for millions of entries
+```
+
+Set algebra reads like the math it is:
+
+```python
+scanned = {22, 80, 443, 8080}
+expected = {22, 80, 443}
+print(scanned & expected)   # {80, 22, 443}  intersection: in both
+print(scanned | expected)   # union: in either
+print(scanned - expected)   # {8080}  difference: unexpected extras
+print(expected - scanned)   # set()  missing services
+```
+
+That difference pattern - "what appeared that I did not expect" - is exactly how you diff two scans, two user lists, or two firewall exports.
+
+Sets are unordered: do not index them or rely on print order. Convert with `sorted(my_set)` when you need stable output.
+
+## Choosing Among list, tuple, set
+
+| Need | Use |
+| --- | --- |
+| Ordered collection that changes | `list` |
+| Fixed-shape record, dict key | `tuple` |
+| Membership tests, dedupe, set math | `set` |
+| Key-value lookup | `dict` |
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Writing `(1)` for a one-element tuple - that is just `1`; write `(1,)`.
+- Expecting set order to be stable or meaningful.
+- Putting a mutable list inside a set (`TypeError: unhashable type`) - use a tuple.
+- Using a list for `in` checks inside a big loop when a set would be O(1).
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Tuples are immutable ordered records; they unpack cleanly and can serve as dict keys.
+- Sets deduplicate and answer membership in O(1); `&`, `|`, `-` perform diff-style analysis.
+- Match the structure to the job; reach for `namedtuple` when positions lose meaning.
+

 ## Learning Objectives

 ## Introduction
 
-Before coding, identify inputs, outputs, constraints, and examples. Write pseudocode, choose data structures, implement, test edge cases, and refactor for readability. Break large tasks into smaller functions.
+Beginners stare at a blank file; experienced programmers shrink the problem until the code is obvious. **Decomposition** is the skill: split a task into stages small enough that each one is a short function with a single job.
 
-## Core Concepts
+The universal shape of a script:
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```text
+INPUT  ->  TRANSFORM  ->  OUTPUT
+(read)     (compute)      (report)
+```
+
+Name those three before writing anything. "Parse the auth log and report brute-force sources" becomes:
+
+```text
+INPUT:     lines from /var/log/auth.log
+TRANSFORM: keep 'Failed password' lines, extract the source IP, count per IP
+OUTPUT:    top 5 IPs with counts, printed as a table
+```
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## Contracts Before Bodies
 
-Problem: count failed statuses.
-Pseudocode:
-1. Set count to zero.
-2. Visit each status.
-3. If it equals "failed", increase count.
-4. Return count.
+Write the function signature and docstring first. The contract - what goes in, what comes out - forces the design:
 
 ```python
-def count_failed(statuses):
-    return sum(1 for status in statuses if status == "failed")
+def extract_ip(line):
+    """Return the source IPv4 address from a 'Failed password' line, or None."""
+
+def count_by_ip(lines):
+    """Return a dict mapping IP -> failure count for the given lines."""
+
+def report_top(counts, limit=5):
+    """Print the `limit` highest-count IPs as an aligned table."""
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Now each body is a five-line problem. Test each in the REPL with one hand-made line before connecting them:
 
-Solve: largest of three numbers, count vowels, identify duplicate values, and count events by severity. For each, write inputs, outputs, pseudocode, and three tests.
+```python
+line = "Oct 10 03:14:15 srv sshd[812]: Failed password for root from 203.0.113.7 port 51122 ssh2"
+print(extract_ip(line))   # 203.0.113.7 - verified before moving on
+```

+## Worked Example: Wiring It Together
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+def extract_ip(line):
+    if "Failed password" not in line:
+        return None
+    marker = " from "
+    if marker not in line:
+        return None
+    return line.split(marker)[1].split()[0]
+
+def count_by_ip(lines):
+    counts = {}
+    for line in lines:
+        ip = extract_ip(line)
+        if ip:
+            counts[ip] = counts.get(ip, 0) + 1
+    return counts
+
+def report_top(counts, limit=5):
+    for ip, n in sorted(counts.items(), key=lambda kv: kv[1], reverse=True)[:limit]:
+        print(f"{ip:<16} {n:>6}")
+```
+
+```python
+counts = count_by_ip(open("auth.log"))
+report_top(counts)
+```
+
+```text
+203.0.113.7        412
+198.51.100.23       97
+```
+
+Each function is testable alone, swappable (regex later), and reusable. That is the payoff of decomposing first.
+
+## Pseudocode and Rubber-Duck Debugging
+
+When a stage still feels big, write pseudocode comments and fill them in:
+
+```python
+# for each line
+#     skip unless it's a failure
+#     pull out the IP
+#     bump that IP's counter
+```
+
+Explaining the plan line by line - to a comment block, a colleague, or the classic rubber duck - exposes the gaps while they are still cheap.

-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Writing the whole script top to bottom before running any of it; the first run then fails in five places at once.
+- Functions that secretly do two stages (parse *and* print), which cannot be tested or reused.
+- Designing in your head for ten minutes instead of writing one example input and its expected output.

-# Testing and Big-O Intuition
+# Testing Basics and Big-O Thinking
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Test functions with assertions and a case matrix (normal, boundary, invalid).
+- Estimate how a solution scales using big-O notation.
+- Choose the simpler algorithm when it is fast enough, and know when it is not.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Test normal, empty, boundary, and invalid inputs. `assert` checks development assumptions but is not a security validation mechanism. Big-O describes growth: O(1) constant, O(n) linear, O(n²) quadratic, O(log n) logarithmic. A list membership check is generally O(n); set membership is average-case O(1).
+"Works on my input" is not tested. A minimal testing habit has two parts: **assertions** that check expected results automatically, and a **case matrix** that covers normal, boundary, and invalid inputs.
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+def is_valid_port(p):
+    return isinstance(p, int) and 1 <= p <= 65535
+
+cases = [
+    (22, True),       # normal
+    (1, True),        # lower boundary
+    (65535, True),    # upper boundary
+    (0, False),       # just outside
+    (65536, False),
+    ("443", False),   # wrong type
+]
+for given, expected in cases:
+    assert is_valid_port(given) == expected, f"failed for {given!r}"
+print("all tests passed")
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```text
+all tests passed
+```
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Run the file; silence-free output means every case passed. Real projects graduate to `pytest` (same asserts, nicer reports, auto-discovery of `test_*.py` files), but the habit starts here: **write the cases before or right after the function, and keep them.**
 
-```python
-def is_even(number):
-    return number % 2 == 0
+## Big-O: How Cost Grows
+
+Big-O describes how runtime or memory grows with input size n:
 
-assert is_even(4)
-assert not is_even(5)
+| Notation | Name | Example |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| O(1) | constant | dict/set lookup, list append |
+| O(log n) | logarithmic | binary search in a sorted list |
+| O(n) | linear | one pass over a list, `in` on a list |
+| O(n log n) | linearithmic | good sorting (`sorted`) |
+| O(n^2) | quadratic | comparing every item with every other |
+
+```python
+# O(n^2): does any pair sum to target?
+def pair_sum_slow(items, target):
+    for a in items:
+        for b in items:
+            if a + b == target:
+                return True
+    return False
+
+# O(n): the same answer with a set
+def pair_sum_fast(items, target):
+    seen = set()
+    for a in items:
+        if target - a in seen:
+            return True
+        seen.add(a)
+    return False
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+At n = 100 the difference is invisible. At n = 1,000,000 the quadratic version does ~10^12 operations (hours) while the set version does ~10^6 (a blink). Log files, packet captures, and password lists reach those sizes casually, which is why the instinct matters for security tooling.
 
-Test a port validator at 1, 65535, 0, 65536, and a wrong type. Explain how a nested loop may scale as input grows.
+## Rules of Thumb
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+1. **Make it correct, then make it fast** - and only if measurement says it is slow (`time.perf_counter()` around the hot part).
+2. Replacing a list with a set or dict for lookups is the most common real-world optimization.
+3. Nested loops over the same data are the quadratic smell; ask whether a set, dict, or sort kills the inner loop.
+4. Reading a whole file when you could stream it line by line is an O(n) memory choice that bites first.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Testing only the happy path; boundary values (0, 1, empty, max) and wrong types are where bugs live.
+- Optimizing before measuring, and making the code worse for no real gain.
+- Quadratic loops over large logs "because it worked on the sample."
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Assert expected results over a matrix of normal, boundary, and invalid cases; keep the tests next to the code.
+- Big-O ranks growth: prefer O(1)/O(n) structures (dict, set) for lookups; beware nested loops over the same data.
+- Measure before optimizing; simple and correct beats clever and fast.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Write a case matrix (at least 5 cases: normal, two boundaries, invalid, wrong-type) for a function `def clamp(x, lo, hi)` that pins x into [lo, hi], then implement it so all cases pass.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+def clamp(x, lo, hi):
+    return max(lo, min(hi, x))
+
+cases = [
+    ((5, 1, 10), 5),     # normal
+    ((1, 1, 10), 1),     # lower boundary
+    ((10, 1, 10), 10),   # upper boundary
+    ((-3, 1, 10), 1),    # below
+    ((99, 1, 10), 10),   # above
+]
+for (x, lo, hi), expected in cases:
+    assert clamp(x, lo, hi) == expected
+print("all tests passed")
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** What is the big-O of `"x" in my_list` inside a `for` loop over n items? Rewrite the pattern to be O(n) overall.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+Each `in` on a list is O(n), inside an n-iteration loop gives O(n^2). Convert once:
+
+```python
+targets = set(target_list)     # O(n) once
+for item in items:             # O(n) loop with O(1) lookups
+    if item in targets:
+        ...
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Time `pair_sum_slow` and `pair_sum_fast` on `list(range(2000))` with target 3999 using `time.perf_counter()` and report both durations.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import time
+
+items = list(range(2000))
+for fn in (pair_sum_slow, pair_sum_fast):
+    start = time.perf_counter()
+    fn(items, 3999)
+    print(fn.__name__, round(time.perf_counter() - start, 4), "s")
+```
+
+```text
+pair_sum_slow 0.1821 s
+pair_sum_fast 0.0001 s
+```
+
+Exact numbers vary; the gap of ~1000x is the lesson.
+</details>
+

+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>

+O(n log n) - Timsort, which is why "sort first, then binary-search or merge" often beats quadratic scans.

 ## Learning Objectives

 ## Introduction
 
-Use `open()` or `Path.open()` with `with` so files close reliably. Modes: `r` read, `w` truncate/write, `a` append, `x` create exclusively, `b` binary. Specify an encoding such as UTF-8 for text. Iterate line by line for large files.
+File I/O is three questions: **which file, which mode, which encoding.** The `with` statement answers a fourth - guaranteeing the file closes even when an error interrupts:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+with open("notes.txt", "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
+    text = f.read()
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Modes: `"r"` read (default, errors if missing), `"w"` write (**truncates the file to empty first**), `"a"` append, `"x"` create but fail if it exists. Add `"b"` for binary (`"rb"`, `"wb"`), which returns `bytes` and skips encoding. Always pass `encoding="utf-8"` for text; the platform default varies and causes mojibake bugs that only appear on other machines.
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## Reading Patterns
 
 ```python
-from pathlib import Path
+with open("app.log", encoding="utf-8") as f:
+    whole = f.read()          # entire file as one string - small files only
+
+with open("app.log", encoding="utf-8") as f:
+    lines = f.readlines()     # list of lines, each keeping its '\n'
+
+with open("app.log", encoding="utf-8") as f:
+    for line in f:            # lazy: constant memory at any file size
+        process(line.rstrip("\n"))
+```
+
+The loop form is the professional default: a 10 GB log streams through with the same memory as a 10 KB one. `readlines()` on a huge file is a self-inflicted outage; `read()` is for files you know are small.
 
-path = Path("notes.txt")
-with path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as file:
-    file.write("first line\n")
+## Writing and Appending
 
-with path.open("a", encoding="utf-8") as file:
-    file.write("second line\n")
+```python
+with open("report.txt", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
+    f.write("scan report\n")
+    print("host: web-1", file=f)     # print can write to any file object
+
+with open("report.txt", "a", encoding="utf-8") as f:
+    f.write("--- second run ---\n")
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Remember: `"w"` destroys existing content the moment `open` succeeds, before a single `write`. If data loss would hurt, write to a temp file and rename, or use `"a"` and headers.

+## When the Path Is Wrong
 
-Create a configuration file, read it line by line, append a record, and explain why `w` would destroy existing contents.
+`open` raises `FileNotFoundError` for a missing read target and `PermissionError` when access is denied. For scripts, catching and saying something useful beats a raw traceback:
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+import sys
+
+try:
+    f = open(sys.argv[1], encoding="utf-8")
+except FileNotFoundError:
+    sys.exit(f"no such file: {sys.argv[1]}")
+except PermissionError:
+    sys.exit(f"permission denied: {sys.argv[1]}")
+
+with f:
+    ...
+```
+
+`sys.exit(message)` prints to stderr and exits with code 1 - exactly what a command-line tool should do. Module 11 goes deeper on exception handling.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Forgetting `with`, leaving files unflushed or locked (especially on Windows).
+- Reading with `readlines()` or `read()` on multi-GB logs.
+- Opening in `"w"` when `"a"` was meant; the old data is already gone.
+- Omitting `encoding="utf-8"` and getting `UnicodeDecodeError` on a teammate's machine.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `with open(path, mode, encoding="utf-8")` is the one safe pattern; modes are `r/w/a/x` plus `b` for binary.
+- Iterate `for line in f` for anything that might be large; `read()`/`readlines()` are for known-small files.
+- `"w"` truncates immediately; catch `FileNotFoundError`/`PermissionError` and exit with a clear message.

 ## Learning Objectives

 ## Introduction
 
-`pathlib.Path` represents portable paths. Use `/` to join components, `.exists()`, `.is_file()`, `.is_dir()`, `.iterdir()`, and `.stat()`. Binary mode (`rb`, `wb`) works with bytes, useful for hashing and file signatures. Common errors include `FileNotFoundError`, `PermissionError`, and decoding errors.
+String-concatenating paths (`folder + "/" + name`) breaks across operating systems and edge cases. `pathlib.Path` is the modern, object-oriented answer:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+from pathlib import Path
+
+logdir = Path("/var/log")
+auth = logdir / "auth.log"          # the / operator joins parts safely
+print(auth.name)                    # auth.log
+print(auth.stem, auth.suffix)       # auth .log
+print(auth.parent)                  # /var/log
+print(auth.exists(), auth.is_file())  # True True
+print(auth.stat().st_size)          # bytes
+```
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+`Path` picks the right separator per OS, so the same code runs on Linux and Windows. Use `Path.home()` and `Path.cwd()` instead of hardcoding.
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## Globbing: Finding Files
 
 ```python

+
+sorted(Path(".").glob("*.txt"), key=lambda p: p.stat().st_mtime)
+```
+
+`glob` vs `rglob` (non-recursive vs recursive) covers most "find me the files" needs without touching `os.walk`.

-# Exceptions and Tracebacks
+# Handling Exceptions
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Catch specific exceptions with `try`/`except` and structure `else`/`finally`.
+- Read a traceback from the bottom up to find the real fault.
+- Decide when to handle, when to convert, and when to let a crash happen.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Syntax errors prevent parsing; runtime errors occur during execution; logical errors produce wrong results. Read tracebacks for the exception type, message, source line, and call stack. Catch specific exceptions such as `ValueError`, `KeyError`, `IndexError`, `FileNotFoundError`, and `ZeroDivisionError`.
-
-## Core Concepts
-
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
-
-## Syntax and Example
+An exception is how Python reports that something could not proceed: a missing file, a bad cast, a dropped connection. Unhandled, it kills the program with a **traceback**; handled well, it becomes a controlled fallback or a clean error message.
 
 ```python
-raw = input("Integer: ")
 try:
-    number = int(raw)
+    port = int(input("Port: "))
 except ValueError:
-    print("Invalid integer")
+    print("that was not a number")
 else:
-    print(number * 2)
+    print(f"port {port} accepted")   # runs only if no exception
 finally:
-    print("Finished")
+    print("(input step finished)")    # always runs
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+The shape rules:
+
+- Catch **specific** exceptions (`ValueError`, `FileNotFoundError`), not everything.
+- Keep the `try` block small - only the lines that can raise the exception you mean to catch.
+- `else` holds code that needs the attempt to have succeeded; `finally` holds cleanup that must happen either way (closing resources, though `with` usually covers that).
+
+## Reading a Traceback
+
+```text
+Traceback (most recent call last):
+  File "scan.py", line 14, in <module>
+    main()
+  File "scan.py", line 9, in main
+    count = int(fields[2])
+IndexError: list index out of range
+```
+
+Read **bottom to top**: the last line says what happened (`IndexError`) and the line above shows where (`int(fields[2])` in `main`, line 9). The frames above it are the call chain that got there. The fix almost always belongs at the bottom frame, not the top.
+
+## Common Exceptions You Will Meet
+
+| Exception | Raised when |
+| --- | --- |
+| `ValueError` | right type, wrong value: `int("abc")` |
+| `TypeError` | wrong type entirely: `"a" + 1` |
+| `KeyError` | missing dict key |
+| `IndexError` | sequence index out of range |
+| `FileNotFoundError` | opening a path that does not exist |
+| `PermissionError` | access denied |
+| `ZeroDivisionError` | `1 / 0` |
+| `ConnectionError`/`TimeoutError` | network failures (also `socket`/`requests` variants) |
+
+## Handle, Convert, or Crash?
 
-Handle invalid integer input, a missing file, and a missing dictionary key. Explain why `except Exception` can hide bugs.
+Three legitimate strategies:
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+1. **Handle and continue** when the failure is expected and survivable: skip a malformed log line, retry a flaky connection.
+2. **Convert to a friendly message** at the program boundary: catch low-level errors and `sys.exit("could not read config: missing file")`.
+3. **Let it crash** when the situation is a programming bug - you *want* the traceback during development. Silencing a bug makes it invisible, not fixed.
+
+```python
+# expected failure, handled locally
+malformed = 0
+for line in open("app.log"):
+    try:
+        code = int(line.split()[8])
+    except (IndexError, ValueError):
+        malformed += 1
+        continue
+```
+
+The anti-pattern to avoid is the bare or blanket catch:
+
+```python
+try:
+    everything()
+except:            # or except Exception: with no logging
+    pass           # errors vanish; bugs become unfindable
+```
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- `except:` or `except Exception: pass` swallowing every error including the typo you just made.
+- Wrapping twenty lines in `try`, so an unexpected error gets "handled" by the wrong `except`.
+- Catching an exception and retrying forever with no backoff or limit.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `try`/`except SpecificError` small and targeted; `else` for success paths, `finally` for guaranteed cleanup.
+- Tracebacks read bottom-up: last line is the fault, the line above it is the location.
+- Handle expected failures, convert at boundaries, and let genuine bugs crash loudly.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Write a loop that asks for a number until the user enters a valid integer, printing a retry message on `ValueError`.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+while True:
+    raw = input("Number: ")
+    try:
+        value = int(raw)
+    except ValueError:
+        print("not an integer, try again")
+        continue
+    break
+print("got", value)
+```
+
+```text
+Number: abc
+not an integer, try again
+Number: 12
+got 12
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Parse `"host,port"` lines from a file, counting and skipping malformed lines without crashing.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+bad = 0
+targets = []
+for line in open("targets.txt"):
+    try:
+        host, port = line.strip().split(",")
+        targets.append((host, int(port)))
+    except ValueError:
+        bad += 1
+print(f"loaded {len(targets)}, skipped {bad} malformed")
+```
+
+Both unpacking failure and `int()` failure raise `ValueError`, so one handler covers the line's shape and content.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Given the traceback from this lesson, state the exception type, the failing line number, and the function it occurred in.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`IndexError: list index out of range`, at line 9 of scan.py, inside `main()`, on the expression `int(fields[2])`. `fields` had fewer than 3 elements, meaning the split produced fewer fields than the parser assumed.
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why is `except Exception: pass` dangerous?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+It hides every failure, including bugs you did not anticipate, making them silent and unfixable. Catch specific exceptions and log or act on them.
+</details>
+
+**What code belongs in the `else` block of a try statement?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Code that should run only when the `try` block raised nothing - keeping it out of `try` prevents accidentally catching its errors.
+</details>
+
+**When is letting a program crash the right choice?**
 
-## Further Practice
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>

+When the exception signals a programming bug rather than an expected runtime condition - the traceback is valuable diagnostic information during development.
+</details>

+So far you caught exceptions; this lesson is about producing them. Functions signal "I cannot do what you asked" with `raise`, which is more useful than returning `None` or -1 because it cannot be silently ignored and it carries a message and type:

-## Syntax and Example
+## Custom Exception Classes
+
+When a module can fail in several distinguishable ways, define your own exception types so callers can catch precisely:
 
 ```python
-class InvalidRecordError(Exception):
-    pass
+class ScanError(Exception):
+    """Base class for scan failures."""
 
-def parse_record(line):
-    fields = line.strip().split(",")
-    if len(fields) != 3:
-        raise InvalidRecordError("expected three fields")
-    return fields
+class HostUnreachable(ScanError):
+    """The target did not respond."""
+
+class ScanTimeout(ScanError):
+    """The scan exceeded its time budget."""
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+```python
+def ping(host):
+    if not reachable(host):
+        raise HostUnreachable(f"no response from {host}")
+
+try:
+    ping("10.0.0.99")
+except ScanTimeout:
+    print("too slow, skipping")
+except ScanError as error:      # catches HostUnreachable too - it is a subclass
+    print("scan failed:", error)
+```
+
+Subclassing a common base lets callers choose granularity: catch the specific case, the family, or let it propagate. Built-in exceptions work the same way (`FileNotFoundError` is a subclass of `OSError`).
+
+## Chaining: Keeping the Root Cause
+
+When you convert a low-level error into a higher-level one, keep the original attached:
+
+```python
+class ConfigError(Exception):
+    pass
 
-Create a validator that raises `ValueError` for an invalid port. Create a custom malformed-record exception and skip only the bad record.
+def load_config(path):
+    try:
+        text = open(path, encoding="utf-8").read()

+```
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+The traceback then shows both: "The above exception was the direct cause of the following exception" - the operator sees your clear message *and* the underlying `PermissionError`. Use `from error` to chain, `from None` to deliberately hide internals.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Returning sentinel values (`None`, `-1`, `"error"`) that callers forget to check; `raise` cannot be ignored by accident.
+- Raising generic `Exception("message")` everywhere, which forces callers into `except Exception` - the blanket catch from lesson 1.
+- Losing the root cause by raising a new exception without `from`.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `raise SomeError("clear message")` signals failure at the exact point it is detected.
+- Custom exception classes under a shared base let callers catch at the granularity they need.
+- `raise NewError(...) from error` preserves the root cause; `from None` hides internals on purpose.

+```python

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+class AuthError(Exception):
+    pass
+
+class BadPassword(AuthError):
+    pass
+
+class AccountLocked(AuthError):
+    pass
+
+def login(user, pw):
+    if user == "locked":
+        raise AccountLocked(f"{user} is locked out")
+    if pw != "s3cret":
+        raise BadPassword("wrong password")
+    return True
+

+    try:
+        login(user, pw)
+        print(user, "logged in")
+    except AccountLocked as e:
+        print(user, "locked:", e)
+    except BadPassword as e:
+        print(user, "failed:", e)
+```
+
+```text
+locked locked: locked is locked out
+alice failed: wrong password
+alice logged in
+```
+</details>

+    pass
+

+```

+```
+</details>

-# Imports and Packages
+# Modules and Imports
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Import standard-library and third-party modules in their proper forms.
+- Create your own module and understand how Python finds it.
+- Choose between `import x`, `from x import y`, and aliased imports.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-A module is a Python file; a package groups modules. Use `import module`, `from module import name`, and aliases with `as`. The standard library ships with Python; third-party packages such as `requests` require installation. Avoid wildcard imports and filenames that shadow standard modules.
+A **module** is just a `.py` file; importing one runs it and gives you access to its names. A **package** is a directory of modules with an `__init__.py`. The standard library ships hundreds of modules - you have already met `sys`, `os`, `re`, `socket`, `pathlib`, `collections`, `datetime`.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## The Three Import Forms
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```python
+import socket
+socket.socket()                    # qualified use: clear where things come from
+
+from pathlib import Path
+Path("app.log")                    # short, but the origin is less visible
+
+import datetime as dt
+dt.datetime.now()                  # alias for long names
+```
+
+Guidelines:
+
+- `import module` is the default; `module.function()` reads like a citation.
+- `from module import name` for one or two heavily used names (`Path`, `Counter`, `defaultdict`).
+- Aliases only for established conventions or long names.
+- Avoid `from module import *`: it dumps unknown names into your file, shadows your own, and makes linters useless.
+
+## Writing Your Own Module
 
-## Syntax and Example
+Any file is importable. Given `netutil.py`:
 
 ```python
-import math
-from pathlib import Path
-import requests as http
+"""Small network helpers."""
 
-print(math.sqrt(25))
-print(Path("notes.txt").suffix)
+COMMON_PORTS = {22: "ssh", 80: "http", 443: "https"}
+
+def service_name(port):
+    return COMMON_PORTS.get(port, "unknown")
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+A sibling file can use it:
+
+```python
+import netutil
+
+print(netutil.service_name(443))   # https
+```
 
-Create `helpers.py` with a normalization function and import it from another file.
+Python searches for modules in this order: the script's own directory, then `PYTHONPATH`, then the installed `site-packages`. That order explains two classic puzzles: a local file named `random.py` or `socket.py` **shadows the standard library** (never name files after stdlib modules), and a module importable from one directory is invisible from another.
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+`import` executes the module top to bottom exactly once; later imports reuse the cached module from `sys.modules`. Top-level code in a module (prints, heavy work) therefore runs on import - keep modules quiet at import time and put executable behavior behind the `if __name__ == "__main__":` guard (next lesson).
+
+## Third-Party Packages
+
+Installed into a venv with pip (module 02), then imported like stdlib:
+
+```bash
+python -m pip install requests
+```
+
+```python
+import requests
+```
+
+The import system does not distinguish them - only their origin differs. When an `ImportError`/`ModuleNotFoundError` appears, the causes are almost always: the package is not installed in *this* environment (`python -m pip show name` to check), the venv is not activated, or a local file shadows the name.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Naming a file `email.py`, `json.py`, or `test.py` and breaking the stdlib import of the same name.
+- `from x import *` and then debugging names that appear from nowhere.
+- Editing a module and re-importing in the same REPL session, wondering why nothing changed (imports are cached; restart the REPL).
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Prefer `import module`; use `from module import name` sparingly; never `import *`.
+- Any `.py` file is a module; search order is script directory, PYTHONPATH, site-packages - so local filenames can shadow the stdlib.
+- Third-party packages install per-venv with pip and import identically to stdlib.

-# __name__ and __main__
+# The if __name__ == "__main__" Guard
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Explain what `__name__` is and how it differs between running and importing a file.
+- Structure scripts with a `main()` function behind the guard.
+- Understand why the guard matters for testing and reuse.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-When a file is executed directly, `__name__` is set to `__main__`; when imported, it receives its module name. Put startup behavior in `main()` and protect it with the main guard to avoid running it during imports.
+Every module has a built-in `__name__` attribute. When you **run** a file directly, Python sets its `__name__` to the string `"__main__"`. When you **import** the same file, `__name__` is the module's real name. This one fact powers the standard script structure:
 
-## Core Concepts
+```python
+def scan(host):
+    return f"results for {host}"
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+def main():
+    print(scan("web-1"))
 
-## Syntax and Example
+if __name__ == "__main__":
+    main()
+```
+
+Run it: `__name__` is `"__main__"`, so `main()` executes. Import it (`import scan_tool` in a test or another script): `__name__` is `"scan_tool"`, the guard is false, and nothing runs - the importing code gets the functions without the side effects.
+
+## Why It Matters
+
+Without the guard, top-level code runs on **every import**:
 
 ```python
+# bad_tool.py
+print("scanning...")        # runs even when another file imports this!
+target = input("host? ")
+```
+
+A test file doing `import bad_tool` would print and then block waiting for input. With the guard, modules are both importable libraries and runnable programs:
+
+```python
+# tool.py
+def add(a, b):
+    return a + b
+
 def main():
-    print("Program started")
+    print(add(2, 3))
 
 if __name__ == "__main__":
     main()
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+```python
+# test_add.py - imports cleanly, no side effects
+from tool import add
+assert add(2, 3) == 5
+print("ok")
+```
+
+This is also the structure you will see in every mature Python codebase: functions and constants at the top, a `main()` orchestrating, the guard at the bottom. `sys.exit(main())` under the guard is a common refinement so `main`'s return value becomes the process exit code.
+
+## argparse Under the Guard
 
-Import a script from another file and confirm that startup output does not run merely because of the import.
+Command-line parsing belongs inside `main` too, so imports never parse arguments:
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+import argparse
+
+def main():
+    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Tiny port checker")
+    parser.add_argument("host", help="target host")
+    parser.add_argument("-p", "--port", type=int, default=80)
+    args = parser.parse_args()
+    print(f"checking {args.host}:{args.port}")
+    return 0
+
+if __name__ == "__main__":
+    raise SystemExit(main())
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 check.py web-1 -p 443
+checking web-1:443
+$ python3 check.py --help
+usage: check.py [-h] [-p PORT] host
+...
+```
+
+`argparse` gives you `--help`, type conversion, and defaults for free - the reason not to hand-roll `sys.argv` parsing once a tool grows past one argument.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- No guard, so tests and imports trigger scans, prompts, or API calls.
+- Putting `argparse` setup at module top level; importing the file then consumes the *test runner's* arguments.
+- Believing the guard makes code private; it only controls execution on import, not access.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `__name__` is `"__main__"` only in the file being run directly.
+- The guard `if __name__ == "__main__": main()` makes a file both an importable module and a runnable program.
+- Keep argument parsing and side effects inside `main()`; return an exit code and pass it to `SystemExit`.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Write `temp.py` with a function `c_to_f(c)` and a guarded `main()` that converts 100 C. Then import `temp` from the REPL and call `c_to_f(0)` - confirm nothing printed on import.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python

+    return c * 9 / 5 + 32
+
+def main():
+    print(c_to_f(100))
+
+if __name__ == "__main__":
+    main()
+```
+
+```text

+```

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import argparse
+
+def main():
+    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Greeter")
+    parser.add_argument("name")
+    parser.add_argument("--shout", action="store_true")
+    args = parser.parse_args()
+    msg = f"Hello, {args.name}!"
+    print(msg.upper() if args.shout else msg)
+    return 0
+
+if __name__ == "__main__":
+    raise SystemExit(main())
+```

+```text

+```

+```

+```text

+```
+
+No conversion output appears because importing `temp` sets its `__name__` to `"temp"`, so the guard is false.
+</details>

+</details>

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+So importing the module (for tests or reuse) never consumes command-line arguments or produces side effects.

+# The os Module: Files, Paths, and Processes
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.

-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Inspect and change the process environment: cwd, env vars, and exit codes.

 ## Introduction
 
-`os` provides operating-system functions. Use `os.getcwd()`, `os.listdir()`, `os.path.exists()`, `os.environ.get()`, and `os.makedirs(..., exist_ok=True)`. Prefer `pathlib` for many path operations. Treat paths and environment variables as potentially missing or untrusted.
+The `os` module is Python's bridge to the operating system: filesystem state, environment variables, and process control. (Prefer `pathlib` for pure path wrangling - module 10 - but `os` still owns environment and process territory.)
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Environment and Working Directory
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```python
+import os
 
-## Syntax and Example
+print(os.getcwd())                    # current working directory
+os.chdir("/tmp")                      # change it
+print(os.environ.get("HOME"))         # read an env var safely
+os.environ["SCAN_PROFILE"] = "fast"   # set one for this process and its children
+
+if os.environ.get("DEBUG"):
+    print("debug mode on")
+```
+
+Environment variables are how tools receive configuration without hardcoding: API tokens, feature flags, target lists. `.get()` avoids `KeyError` on missing variables, and you can supply a default: `os.environ.get("TIMEOUT", "5")` - remember the value is always a string.
+
+## Filesystem Operations
 
 ```python
-import os
-print(os.getcwd())

-home = os.environ.get("HOME") or os.environ.get("USERPROFILE")

-os.makedirs("reports/daily", exist_ok=True)

+os.remove("temp.txt")           # delete a file
+os.path.exists("app.log")
+os.path.getsize("app.log")      # bytes
+os.path.join("logs", "app.log") # path join (pathlib's / is nicer)
 ```

+`os.walk` visits a whole tree when `rglob` is not enough control:

+## Running External Commands: subprocess
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Sometimes the right tool already exists (`nmap`, `git`, `openssl`). `subprocess.run` executes it and captures the result:
+
+```python
+import subprocess
+
+result = subprocess.run(
+    ["nmap", "-p", "22,80", "scanme.nmap.org"],
+    capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=60,
+)
+print(result.returncode)
+print(result.stdout[:200])
+```
+
+Two safety rules:
+
+1. **Always pass a list of arguments**, never a glued-together string with `shell=True`. `subprocess.run(f"nmap {user_input}", shell=True)` is a command-injection hole: a `user_input` of `x; rm -rf ~` runs the second command too. The list form passes each element to the program literally.
+2. **Set a timeout** on anything that touches the network, or one hung host freezes your script.
+
+`check=True` makes a nonzero exit code raise `CalledProcessError` instead of passing silently.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- `shell=True` with string-built commands - the classic injection bug.
+- Assuming the script's cwd is the script's directory; it is wherever the user launched it. Anchor paths to `Path(__file__).parent` when it matters.
+- Reading env vars with `os.environ["KEY"]` and crashing when unset; use `.get` with a default.
+- Not checking `returncode`, so a failed external command looks like success.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `os.environ` configures tools via the environment; `.get()` with defaults keeps it robust.
+- `os.listdir`/`makedirs`/`rename`/`remove`/`walk` cover filesystem jobs; `pathlib` handles paths more pleasantly.
+- `subprocess.run([...], capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=...)` runs external tools; lists and timeouts keep it safe.
+

+# The sys Module: Arguments, Streams, and the Interpreter
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Use `sys.argv`, `sys.stdin/stdout/stderr`, and `sys.exit` correctly.
+- Read piped input like a proper Unix tool.
+- Inspect interpreter facts: version, path, and platform.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-`sys.argv` is a list of command-line strings; index zero is the script name. `sys.exit()` ends the process with a status code. `sys.stdin`, `sys.stdout`, and `sys.stderr` represent standard streams.
+`sys` exposes the interpreter itself: how your program was launched, where its input and output flow, and when it stops. You met `sys.argv` in module 02; this lesson completes the picture with streams and exits.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## The Three Streams
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Every process has stdin, stdout, and stderr. `print()` writes to stdout; errors belong on stderr so pipelines stay clean:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+import sys
+
+print("normal output")
+print("something failed", file=sys.stderr)
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 tool.py > results.txt
+something failed        # still visible: only stdout was redirected
+$ python3 tool.py 2> errors.txt > results.txt   # split them
+```
+
+**Reading piped input** is what makes a script composable with other Unix tools:
 
 ```python
 import sys
-if len(sys.argv) != 2:
-    print("Usage: python tool.py <filename>")
-    raise SystemExit(2)
-print(f"Selected: {sys.argv[1]}")
+
+for line in sys.stdin:
+    if "error" in line.lower():
+        print(line.strip())
+```
+
+```text
+$ cat app.log | python3 only_errors.py
+ERROR disk full
+$ grep . app.log | python3 only_errors.py | wc -l
+41
+```
+
+This one loop turns your script into a filter that plugs into `cat`, `grep`, `tail -f`, and `wc` - the Unix philosophy in five lines. A common refinement accepts a filename argument *or* stdin:
+
+```python
+import sys
+
+source = open(sys.argv[1], encoding="utf-8") if len(sys.argv) > 1 else sys.stdin
+with source:
+    for line in source:
+        ...
+```
+
+## Exits and Interpreter Facts
+
+```python
+sys.exit(0)          # success
+sys.exit("message")  # prints to stderr, exits with code 1
+sys.exit(2)          # convention: usage error
+
+print(sys.version)          # full version string
+print(sys.platform)         # 'linux', 'darwin', 'win32'
+print(sys.executable)       # which interpreter is running this
+print(sys.path[:3])         # module search path (first entries)
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+`sys.platform` gates OS-specific code:
+
+```python
+import sys
 
-Accept one filename argument, print usage if missing, and exit non-zero if the file does not exist.
+if sys.platform == "win32":
+    config_dir = Path.home() / "AppData"
+else:

+```

+And `sys.executable` is the answer to "which Python am I really on?" - worth printing in bug reports and sanity checks inside scripts that must run under a specific interpreter.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Printing errors to stdout, so they contaminate piped data files.
+- Reading all of stdin with `sys.stdin.read()` when line iteration would stream it; the read blocks until EOF on a pipe.
+- Hardcoding OS assumptions (`/etc/...` paths) without a `sys.platform` check.
+- Deep-fiddling `sys.path` to make imports work instead of fixing the project layout or venv.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- stdout is for results, stderr for diagnostics; redirect them independently.
+- `for line in sys.stdin` makes any script a pipeline citizen; support "file argument or stdin" for flexibility.
+- `sys.exit` sets the exit code, and `sys.version`/`platform`/`executable` answer interpreter questions.

-# HTTP and GET Requests
+# HTTP GET with requests
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Make GET requests with parameters, headers, and timeouts.
+- Interpret status codes, headers, and response bodies.
+- Handle network failures without crashing.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-HTTP clients send requests containing a method, target, headers, and optionally a body. Servers return a status code, headers, and a body. 2xx indicates success, 3xx redirection, 4xx client-side issue, and 5xx server-side issue. `requests` is third-party: install with `python -m pip install requests`.
+HTTP is a request-response protocol: the client sends a method, path, headers, and optional body; the server answers with a status code, headers, and a body. Status classes: 2xx success, 3xx redirect, 4xx your fault, 5xx their fault.
 
-## Core Concepts
+`requests` is the friendly third-party HTTP library (the stdlib's `urllib` works but is clunkier). It is the one non-stdlib dependency this course uses:
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```bash
+python -m pip install requests
+```
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## The Minimal Request - and the Timeout Rule
 
 ```python
 import requests
+
+response = requests.get("https://httpbin.org/get", timeout=5)
+print(response.status_code)    # 200
+print(response.headers["content-type"])  # application/json
+print(response.json())         # parsed body, since httpbin answers JSON
+```
+
+**Every request gets a `timeout`.** Without one, `requests` waits forever - one hung server freezes your tool permanently. A connect+read timeout of a few seconds is the norm for scripts.
+
+## Parameters, Headers, and Response Details
+
+```python
+response = requests.get(
+    "https://httpbin.org/get",
+    params={"q": "python", "page": 2},        # becomes ?q=python&page=2
+    headers={"User-Agent": "study-script/1.0"},
+    timeout=5,
+)
+print(response.url)            # https://httpbin.org/get?q=python&page=2
+print(response.text[:80])      # raw body as text
+print(response.elapsed)        # how long it took
+```
+
+Never build query strings by string concatenation (`url + "?" + key + "=" + value`): special characters break or - worse - smuggle extra parameters. `params=` encodes correctly.
+
+Inspect what came back before using it:
+
+```python
+response = requests.get("https://httpbin.org/status/404", timeout=5)
+if response.status_code == 200:
+    data = response.json()
+elif response.status_code == 404:
+    print("not found")
+else:
+    response.raise_for_status()   # raises HTTPError for 4xx/5xx
+```
+
+`response.json()` raises `requests.exceptions.JSONDecodeError` when the body is not JSON - check `content-type` or wrap it.
+
+## Handling Failure
+
+Networks fail in ways that are not HTTP errors at all. Catch the request exceptions:
+
+```python
+import requests
+
 try:
-    response = requests.get(
-        "https://httpbin.org/get",
-        params={"topic": "python"},
-        timeout=5,
-    )
-    print(response.status_code)
-    print(response.headers.get("Content-Type"))
+    response = requests.get("https://unreachable.example", timeout=5)
+    response.raise_for_status()
 except requests.Timeout:
-    print("Timed out")
+    print("server too slow")
+except requests.ConnectionError:
+    print("host unreachable or DNS failed")
+except requests.HTTPError as error:
+    print("bad status:", error.response.status_code)
 except requests.RequestException as error:
-    print(f"Request failed: {error}")
+    print("request failed:", error)
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
-
-Use a local or instructor-authorized endpoint. Add query parameters, display selected headers, and handle timeouts. Do not rely on a public test service being available.
-
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Order matters: the specific exceptions come before the base `RequestException`, which catches everything `requests` raises.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- No timeout - the number one production hang in Python scripts.
+- Concatenating query strings by hand instead of using `params`.
+- Calling `.json()` on error pages or non-JSON bodies without checking.
+- Ignoring status codes; a 404 page is still a "successful" HTTP exchange unless you check.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `requests.get(url, params=..., headers=..., timeout=...)`; always set a timeout.
+- Check `status_code` (or `raise_for_status`) before trusting the body; `.json()` only on JSON.
+- Catch `Timeout`, `ConnectionError`, `HTTPError`, then `RequestException` as the catch-all.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** GET `https://httpbin.org/get` with a custom User-Agent and print the JSON's `headers` field to confirm the server saw it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python

+

+                 headers={"User-Agent": "my-first-script/0.1"}, timeout=5)

+```

+```
+</details>

+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import requests
+
+try:
+    r = requests.get("https://httpbin.org/status/418", timeout=5)
+    r.raise_for_status()
+except requests.HTTPError as e:
+    print(f"server said {e.response.status_code} - yes, 418 is 'I'm a teapot'")
+```

+```text
+server said 418 - yes, 418 is 'I'm a teapot'
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Request `https://httpbin.org/delay/10` (which sleeps 10 seconds) with `timeout=2` and catch the timeout. Note how long your script actually waited.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import time
+import requests
+
+start = time.perf_counter()
+try:
+    requests.get("https://httpbin.org/delay/10", timeout=2)
+except requests.Timeout:
+    print(f"gave up after {time.perf_counter() - start:.1f}s")
+```
+
+```text
+gave up after 2.0s
+```
+
+Without the timeout, the script would wait the full 10 seconds (or forever on a dead host).
+</details>

+```python

+
+# JSON body (APIs):

+```

+

+Note `true` lowercase on the wire - JSON is not Python. `dumps`/`loads` work on strings; `dump`/`load` work on files. JSON supports only objects, arrays, strings, numbers, booleans, and null - Python tuples come back as lists, and sets/datetimes need converting first.

+## Sessions: Cookies, Headers, and Keep-Alive
 
-POST harmless sample data to a local test endpoint. Handle timeout, HTTP errors, invalid JSON, and unexpected response structure.
+A `Session` persists cookies across requests, applies shared headers, and reuses the TCP connection (faster for many requests to one host):
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+s = requests.Session()
+s.headers.update({"User-Agent": "audit-script/1.0"})
+
+s.post("https://httpbin.org/cookies/set/session/abc123", timeout=5)
+r = s.get("https://httpbin.org/cookies", timeout=5)
+print(r.json())   # {'cookies': {'session': 'abc123'}} - cookie persisted
+```
+
+Login flows work the same way: POST credentials once, and the session carries the authenticated cookie to every later request. When scraping or testing several pages on one site, a session is both faster and more polite.

 ## Key Takeaways

+- `json=` for API JSON bodies, `data=` for form posts; the server cares about the difference.
+- `response.json()` parses replies; the `json` module converts strings/files with `dumps`/`loads`/`dump`/`load`.
+- `requests.Session` persists cookies and headers and reuses connections - use it for multi-request flows.

-# Sockets, TCP, and UDP
+# TCP and UDP: The socket Module
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Explain what a socket is and how TCP differs from UDP.
+- Create both socket types and resolve hostnames.
+- Send and receive raw bytes with correct encoding and timeouts.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-A socket is a communication endpoint. An IP address identifies a host/interface; a port identifies an application endpoint. TCP is connection-oriented and provides a reliable ordered byte stream. UDP sends datagrams without guarantees of delivery or ordering. Use localhost for exercises.
+A **socket** is the operating system's endpoint for network communication - the thing a program sends bytes into and reads bytes out of. Everything higher-level (HTTP, SSH, DNS) is a convention about what those bytes mean. Working at the socket level teaches you what `requests` and browsers do underneath, and it is how banners are grabbed, services are fingerprinted, and custom protocols are spoken.
 
-## Core Concepts
+**TCP** (`SOCK_STREAM`) is a connection: reliable, ordered, with a handshake. Like a phone call - web, SSH, and email run on it. **UDP** (`SOCK_DGRAM`) is fire-and-forget datagrams: no connection, no guarantee, minimal overhead - DNS, VoIP, and games prefer speed over certainty.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## TCP: Connect, Send, Receive
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+import socket
 
-| TCP | UDP |
-|---|---|
-| Connection setup | No connection setup |
-| Reliable ordered byte stream | No delivery/order guarantee |
-| `SOCK_STREAM` | `SOCK_DGRAM` |
+s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
+s.settimeout(5)                          # never block forever
+s.connect(("example.com", 80))           # TCP handshake happens here
+s.sendall(b"HEAD / HTTP/1.0\r\nHost: example.com\r\n\r\n")
 
-## Practical Exercise
+data = b""
+while True:
+    chunk = s.recv(4096)
+    if not chunk:                        # b"" means the peer closed
+        break
+    data += chunk
+s.close()
+print(data.decode(errors="replace")[:200])
+```
 
-Resolve `localhost`; explain host versus port; choose TCP or UDP for a reliable ordered request/response service.
+```text
+HTTP/1.0 200 OK
+Content-Type: text/html
+...
+```
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+Points that bite beginners:
+
+- Sockets move **bytes**, so encode on send (`"text".encode()`) and decode on receive.
+- HTTP needs `\r\n\r\n` to end the headers and a `Host` header (HTTP/1.1 requires it).
+- `recv(n)` returns **up to** n bytes; you loop until it returns empty. There is no "read the whole response" call - TCP is a stream with no built-in message boundaries.
+
+## UDP and Name Resolution
+
+UDP has no connect handshake; you address every datagram:
+
+```python
+s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
+s.settimeout(3)
+s.sendto(b"\x00", ("8.8.8.8", 53))       # (not a valid DNS query, just the shape)
+```
+
+Resolution turns names into addresses - the first step of any scanner:
+
+```python
+print(socket.gethostbyname("example.com"))        # 93.184.216.34
+print(socket.gethostbyname_ex("example.com"))     # (name, aliases, [all IPs])
+```
+
+And `connect_ex`, seen in module 01, is the port-scanner primitive: it returns 0 on success instead of raising.
+
+## The with Statement and Cleanup
+
+Sockets hold OS resources. `with socket.socket(...) as s:` closes reliably, and for servers (next lesson) `s.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)` avoids "address already in use" after restarts.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- No `settimeout`: one silent host freezes the script forever.
+- Assuming one `recv` gets the whole reply; streams need a read loop.
+- Sending str instead of bytes (`TypeError: a bytes-like object is required`).
+- Scanning hosts without authorization - the lesson-01 rules apply to every socket you open.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- TCP (`SOCK_STREAM`) is a reliable connected stream; UDP (`SOCK_DGRAM`) is connectionless datagrams.
+- Encode/decode at the boundary, always set timeouts, and loop `recv` until it returns empty bytes.
+- `gethostbyname` resolves names; `connect_ex` returns a status code and is the scanner's building block.
+
