-# TCP Client and Server
+# Building a TCP Client and Server
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Write a minimal TCP server: bind, listen, accept.
+- Write a matching client and run both on one machine.
+- Structure a tiny request-response protocol, including framing.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-A TCP server binds, listens, and accepts a client. The accepted socket communicates with that client. TCP is a byte stream, so applications must define message framing. Use timeouts and context managers to close sockets.
+The client-server pair is where sockets click: the server binds to a port and waits; the client connects; both then speak an agreed protocol. Building one yourself - on localhost, which is always an authorized target - teaches more about networking than a week of reading.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## The Server: bind, listen, accept
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
-
-## Syntax and Example
-
-Server:
 ```python
 import socket
+
 with socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM) as server:
     server.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
-    server.bind(("127.0.0.1", 50007))
-    server.listen(1)
+    server.bind(("127.0.0.1", 9000))
+    server.listen(5)                     # backlog of pending connections
+    print("listening on 127.0.0.1:9000")
+    while True:
+        conn, addr = server.accept()     # blocks until a client arrives
+        with conn:
+            data = conn.recv(1024)
+            if not data:
+                continue
+            message = data.decode().strip().upper()
+            conn.sendall(message.encode() + b"\n")
+```
+
+`bind` claims the address, `listen` queues incoming connections, and `accept` returns a **new** socket (`conn`) for that specific client plus its address. The listening socket keeps waiting for the next client.
+
+## The Client
+
+```python
+import socket
+
+with socket.create_connection(("127.0.0.1", 9000), timeout=5) as s:
+    s.sendall(b"hello server\n")
+    print("reply:", s.recv(1024).decode().strip())
+```
+
+Run the server in one terminal, the client in another:
+
+```text
+$ python3 server.py          # terminal 1
+listening on 127.0.0.1:9000
+$ python3 client.py          # terminal 2
+reply: HELLO SERVER
+```
+
+You just built a working echo-uppercase service. Test it by hand too: `nc 127.0.0.1 9000`, type a line, see it return uppercased. `nc` (netcat) is the universal socket debugger.
+
+## Framing: Where Does a Message End?
+
+TCP is a byte stream - "hello" and "server" sent separately may arrive merged. Protocols solve this with **framing**: length prefixes, or delimiters like newline (what our example assumes). A robust newline reader buffers until it sees `\n`:
+
+```python
+def read_line(conn):
+    data = b""
+    while not data.endswith(b"\n"):
+        chunk = conn.recv(1024)
+        if not chunk:
+            break
+        data += chunk
+    return data
+```
+
+Our simple server handles one client at a time - a second client waits until the first disconnects. Threading (`threading.Thread(target=handle, args=(conn,))`) is the next step when concurrent clients matter.
+
+## Common Mistakes
+
+- Forgetting `SO_REUSEADDR`, then "Address already in use" on every quick restart.
+- Assuming one `sendall` equals one `recv` on the other side; without framing, messages merge or split.
+- Binding a test server to `0.0.0.0` (all interfaces) when `127.0.0.1` would do - do not expose unfinished services to the network.
+- Not handling client disconnect (`recv` returning empty), crashing the server on the first Ctrl-C from a client.
+
+## Key Takeaways
+
+- Servers: socket, `SO_REUSEADDR`, bind, listen, then loop on `accept` which returns a per-client connection socket.
+- TCP is a stream: messages need framing (delimiters or length prefixes) and `recv` needs a loop.
+- Test locally with two terminals and `nc`; bind to `127.0.0.1` unless the network genuinely needs the service.
+

+```

+Any uppercase echo back means the loop works; Ctrl-C the server to stop it.

+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+count = 0
+while True:
     conn, addr = server.accept()

 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Read unfamiliar code top-down by purpose, not line by line.
+- Trace execution by hand with a variable table.
+- Use strategic prints to follow data through a program.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Read imports and constants, find the entry point, follow function calls, track variables, and identify side effects such as file writes, network requests, or global-state changes. Imports execute module-level statements, so inspect them before running unfamiliar code.
+You will read more code than you write - inherited scripts, library internals, other people's tools. Reading well is a skill with a method, not a talent.
 
-## Core Concepts
+**Pass 1: purpose.** Read the docstrings, the `main()` function, and the names. Build a one-sentence answer to "what does this program do?" Do not read function bodies yet.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+**Pass 2: flow.** Follow `main()` and note the functions it calls in order. Sketch the input-to-output pipeline: where data enters, what transforms it, where it leaves.
 
-## Syntax and Example
+**Pass 3: detail.** Only now read individual function bodies, starting with the ones on the data's path. Skip error handling and rare branches on the first read.

+## Hand-Tracing with a Variable Table
+
+When a function misbehaves, execute it on paper. Columns are variables, rows are steps:
 
 ```python
-def add_tax(price, rate):
-    total = price * (1 + rate)
-    return total
+def dedupe_preserve_order(items):
+    seen = set()
+    result = []
+    for item in items:
+        if item not in seen:
+            seen.add(item)
+            result.append(item)
+    return result
+```
+
+Tracing `dedupe_preserve_order(["b", "a", "b"])`:
+
+| step | item | seen | result |
+| --- | --- | --- | --- |
+| 1 | "b" | {b} | [b] |
+| 2 | "a" | {b, a} | [b, a] |
+| 3 | "b" | (already in seen, skip) | [b, a] |
 
-print(add_tax(100, 0.1))
+Return value: `["b", "a"]`. The table forces you to be the interpreter; wrong mental models show up immediately as rows you cannot explain.
+
+## Strategic Printing
+
+The fastest debugger is a well-placed print - used with intent, not sprayed everywhere:
+
+```python
+def count_by_ip(lines):
+    counts = {}
+    for i, line in enumerate(lines):
+        ip = extract_ip(line)
+        if ip is None:
+            print(f"DEBUG line {i}: no IP in {line[:60]!r}")   # temporary
+            continue
+        counts[ip] = counts.get(ip, 0) + 1
+    return counts
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Rules that keep print-debugging useful:
 
-Trace values line by line. Draw a call sequence for a function calling two helpers. List side effects in an unfamiliar script before executing it.
+- Print **labels and values** (`f"DEBUG {i=} {ip=}"` - the `=` specifier prints name and value together), not bare numbers you cannot attribute.
+- Print at **boundaries**: where data enters a function, after each transformation.
+- Mark temporary prints (`DEBUG` prefix) so they are trivial to find and remove - or use `logging` (module 16 lesson 2) from the start.
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+## Reading Error Output
+
+Combine reading with the traceback skill from module 11: locate the failing line, then hand-trace backward to where the bad value was born. The error is where it *surfaced*; the bug is where the value went wrong - often several frames earlier.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Reading line 1 to line 500 in order and drowning; purpose first, details last.
+- Tracing in your head instead of on paper for anything longer than five lines.
+- Twenty unlabeled prints whose output cannot be told apart.
+- Fixing the line the traceback names without checking where its inputs came from.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Read in passes: purpose, flow, then detail; let `main()` be your map.
+- Hand-trace with a variable table when behavior is surprising.
+- Print with labels at data boundaries, using `f"{var=}"`, and clean up after.
+

+</details>

+# Debugging Tools: pdb and logging
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Step through running code with `pdb` breakpoints.
+- Replace scattered prints with the `logging` module's levels and handlers.
+- Know when prints, logging, or the debugger is the right tool.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Use tracebacks to find the failing line and call stack. Temporary `print()` statements reveal state but must not expose secrets. Run `python -m pdb script.py`; useful commands include `n`, `s`, `c`, `p expression`, and `q`. `breakpoint()` pauses execution. Assertions are development checks, not security validation.
+Prints answer "what is this value?"; a debugger answers "what is *everything*, right now?"; logging answers "what happened last Tuesday in production?" This lesson adds `pdb` and `logging` to the print-debugging you already know.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## pdb: Pause and Inspect
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+Drop a breakpoint into code and run it:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+def count_by_ip(lines):
+    counts = {}
+    for line in lines:
+        ip = extract_ip(line)
+        breakpoint()          # execution pauses here
+        counts[ip] = counts.get(ip, 0) + 1
+    return counts
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 analyzer.py
+> /home/you/analyzer.py(6)count_by_ip()
+-> counts[ip] = counts.get(ip, 0) + 1
+(Pdb) p ip
+'203.0.113.7'
+(Pdb) p counts
+{}
+(Pdb) n          # next line
+(Pdb) c          # continue to next breakpoint
+(Pdb) l          # list source around here
+(Pdb) q          # quit
+```
+
+The essential commands: `p expr` (print), `n` (next line), `s` (step into a call), `c` (continue), `l` (list code), `w` (where am I - the stack), `q` (quit). You can also start post-mortem debugging after a crash: `python3 -m pdb script.py` drops you into the debugger at the unhandled exception, with all variables still alive.
+
+## logging: Prints That Grow Up
+
+`print` cannot be turned off, leveled, or redirected without editing code. `logging` can:
 
 ```python
-assert isinstance(records, list)
+import logging
+
+logging.basicConfig(
+    level=logging.INFO,
+    format="%(asctime)s %(levelname)-7s %(message)s",
+)
+log = logging.getLogger("analyzer")
+
+log.debug("raw line: %r", line)        # hidden at INFO level
+log.info("processed %d lines", n)
+log.warning("skipped malformed line %d", i)
+log.error("could not open %s", path)
 ```
-Run:
-```bash
-python -m pdb script.py
+
+```text
+2024-10-10 03:14:15 INFO    processed 1200 lines
+2024-10-10 03:14:15 WARNING skipped malformed line 87
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+The levels - DEBUG < INFO < WARNING < ERROR < CRITICAL - let one flag control verbosity: `level=logging.DEBUG` during development, `WARNING` in production, with zero code changes. Send output to a file by adding `filename="analyzer.log"` to `basicConfig`, and errors gain tracebacks with `log.exception("failed")` inside an except block.
 
-Debug a wrong counter, use pdb to inspect a variable, and add regression tests. Explain why assertions should not validate untrusted user input.
+## Choosing the Tool
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+- **One quick value, right now, code you'll delete:** print.
+- **"Something is wrong and I don't know where":** pdb breakpoint at the suspicious spot; inspect everything.
+- **Anything that must keep working next month, especially servers and cron jobs:** logging.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Leaving `breakpoint()` in committed code (CI and users will hit it).
+- Logging secrets: passwords, tokens, and full request bodies do not belong in logs.
+- Using f-strings in logging calls (`log.info(f"{x}")`) - the `%s` style defers formatting until the message is actually emitted.
+- Setting no level and wondering where DEBUG messages went (default is WARNING).
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `breakpoint()` + `p/n/s/c/l/w/q` in pdb inspects a live, paused program; `python3 -m pdb` catches crashes post-mortem.
+- `logging` levels control verbosity without code edits; `log.exception` captures tracebacks; logs can go to files.
+- Prints for the moment, the debugger for confusion, logging for anything that persists.
+

 ## Learning Objectives

 ## Introduction
 
-Debug by reproducing the problem, identifying the cause, making one focused change, and testing again. Example bug: `count =+ 1` assigns positive one; `count += 1` increments. Validate dictionary keys and types before using them.
+This lab is deliberately broken code. Each snippet has one or more bugs of a species you have met. For each: **predict** what it does wrong, **run** it to confirm, **fix** it, and **prove** the fix with a test. That loop - reproduce, diagnose, fix, verify - is the whole job.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Bug 1: The Quiet Shadow
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```python
+def total_open(ports):
+    total = 0
+    for port in ports:
+        total = port        # supposed to accumulate
+    return total
+
+print(total_open([22, 80, 443]))   # prints 443, expected 545
+```
+
+Diagnosis: `total = port` overwrites instead of accumulating. The symptom ("answer is the last item") points at the loop body.
+
+Fix: `total += port`. Verify: `assert total_open([22, 80, 443]) == 545` and `assert total_open([]) == 0`.
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## Bug 2: The Off-by-One
 
-Broken:
 ```python
-def count_errors(lines):
-    count = 0
-    for line in lines:
-        if "ERROR" in line:
-            count =+ 1
-    return count
+def first_n(items, n):
+    return [items[i] for i in range(1, n + 1)]
+
+print(first_n(["a", "b", "c"], 2))   # IndexError
 ```
-Correct:
+
+Diagnosis: `range(1, n + 1)` starts at 1 (skipping index 0) and reaches `n` (out of range for short lists). Hand-trace the indexes: for n=2 it touches 1 and 2, but "first 2" means indexes 0 and 1.
+
+Fix: `return items[:n]` (slicing also tolerates n larger than the list). Verify with n = 0, 2, 10 on a 3-item list.
+
+## Bug 3: The Wrong Type
+
+```python
+port = input("port: ")
+if 1 <= port <= 65535:
+    print("valid")
+```
+
+Diagnosis: `input` returns a string; comparing `int <= str` raises `TypeError` in Python 3. Even before that, `"80" <= "65535"` style comparisons would be lexicographic, not numeric.
+
+Fix: convert and validate -
+
 ```python
-def count_errors(lines):
-    count = 0
-    for line in lines:
-        if "ERROR" in line:
-            count += 1
-    return count
+raw = input("port: ")
+if raw.isdigit() and 1 <= int(raw) <= 65535:
+    print("valid")
 ```

+Verify with `22`, `0`, `99999`, `abc`.

+## Bug 4: The Mutable Default
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+def add_finding(findings, found=[]):
+    found.append(findings)
+    return found
 
-## Common Mistakes
+print(add_finding("xss"))     # ['xss']
+print(add_finding("sqli"))    # ['xss', 'sqli']  <- remembers the last call!

-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+Diagnosis: the default list is created once at definition time and shared (module 07).
+
+Fix:
+
+```python
+def add_finding(finding, found=None):
+    if found is None:
+        found = []
+    found.append(finding)
+    return found
+```
+
+Verify: two fresh calls each return one item.
+
+## Bug 5: The Unreachable Branch
+
+```python
+def severity(count):
+    if count > 0:
+        return "low"
+    elif count > 10:
+        return "high"
+    return "none"
+```
+
+Diagnosis: branch order. Every count > 10 is also > 0, so "high" never fires. Hand-trace count = 50 to see it return "low".
+
+Fix: test the strictest condition first (`count > 10` before `count > 0`). Verify: 0 -> "none", 5 -> "low", 50 -> "high".
+
+## Verification Habit
+
+Bundle the fixes with their tests so regressions announce themselves:
+
+```python
+assert total_open([22, 80, 443]) == 545
+assert first_n(["a", "b", "c"], 2) == ["a", "b"]
+assert first_n(["a"], 10) == ["a"]
+assert add_finding("a") == ["a"] and add_finding("b") == ["b"]
+assert severity(50) == "high"
+print("all fixes verified")
+```
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Reproduce, diagnose (traceback + hand-trace), fix, then prove with assertions.
+- The five species: overwrite-instead-of-accumulate, off-by-one, str/int confusion, mutable defaults, misordered branches.
+- Keep the tests; the same bug families come back.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Fix: `def average(nums): return sum(nums) / len(nums)` crashes on an empty list. Decide the empty-case behavior yourself and test it.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+def average(nums):
+    if not nums:
+        return 0
+    return sum(nums) / len(nums)
+
+assert average([]) == 0
+assert average([2, 4]) == 3.0
+```
+
+(Returning 0 is one defensible choice; raising ValueError with a message is another - the point is to decide explicitly.)
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** This countdown never ends. Find why and fix it:
+
+```python
+n = 10
+while n != 0:
+    print(n)
+    n -= 2
+```
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+Starting at 10 and stepping by 2, `n` hits ..., 4, 2, 0 - actually that one terminates. The trap is `n -= 2` from an odd start (or `n -= 1` from a negative start): it skips 0 forever. Robust fix:
+
+```python
+n = 10
+while n > 0:
+    print(n)
+    n -= 2
+```
+
+Inequalities as exit tests tolerate any start value.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** `greet` prints the wrong name. Explain and fix:
+
+```python
+def greet(name="world"):
+    print("hello", name)
+
+name = "alice"
+greet()
+```
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+The module-level `name = "alice"` is never passed in; the default `"world"` is used. Fix by passing it explicitly: `greet(name)`. Defaults only apply when the caller supplies nothing.
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why does "the answer equals the last item" suggest an overwrite bug?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Because assignment inside the loop replaces the accumulator each pass, leaving only the final iteration's value - accumulation requires `+=` (or append).
+</details>
+
+**Why is `items[:n]` a better "first n" than a range-based index loop?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Slicing starts at 0 and tolerates n beyond the list length - no off-by-one and no IndexError.
+</details>
+
+**What is the verification step after each fix in this lab?**
 
-## Further Practice
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-Modify the example, explain each line in your own words, and link the exercise to the next module.
+An assertion (or case matrix) covering the original failing input plus boundaries, kept with the code so regressions surface immediately.
+</details>

-# Basic TCP Port Scanner
+# Building a TCP Port Scanner
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Combine sockets, loops, and functions into a working port scanner.
+- Add timeouts, concurrency limits, and clean output.
+- Scope scanning to authorized targets only.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Use only localhost or an explicitly authorized lab target. This beginner tool checks a small configurable range sequentially; it is not a replacement for Nmap. A successful TCP connection means the handshake succeeded at that moment. A failed attempt does not prove the port is closed: filtering, routing, or timeout may explain it.
+A port scanner asks one question per port: does a TCP connection succeed? You already own every piece - `connect_ex` (module 01), timeouts (module 15), argument parsing (module 12), aligned output (module 03). This lesson assembles them.
 
-## Core Concepts
+**Authorization first:** run this against `scanme.nmap.org` (provided by the Nmap project for testing), your own machines, or lab VMs. Nothing else, ever.
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+## The Core Loop
 
-## Syntax and Example
+```python
+import socket
+
+def check_port(host, port, timeout=1.0):
+    with socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM) as s:
+        s.settimeout(timeout)
+        return s.connect_ex((host, port)) == 0
+
+def scan(host, ports):
+    open_ports = []
+    for port in ports:
+        if check_port(host, port):
+            open_ports.append(port)
+    return open_ports
+```
+
+That is a complete scanner in ten lines. `connect_ex` returns 0 when the handshake completes; anything else (refused, filtered, timed out) counts as closed for this purpose.
+

+## Speed: Threads with a Bound
 
-Change to a three-port localhost range. Start a local test service on one port and observe the result. Keep the range small and do not scan third-party hosts.
+One port per second times 1000 ports is slow. Threads overlap the waiting; a `ThreadPoolExecutor` caps how many run at once:
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+```python
+from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
+
+with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=100) as pool:
+    results = pool.map(lambda p: (p, check_port(ip, p)), ports)
+open_ports = [p for p, ok in results if ok]
+```
+
+One hundred concurrent connection attempts is a reasonable default for a lab. Hundreds more is how you trip rate limits and IDS alerts - noisy scanning is an operational mistake, not a flex. Real tools (nmap) add timing templates, service detection, and SYN scanning; knowing the TCP-connect version's limits tells you why those exist.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- No timeout per connection, so filtered ports stall the scan.
+- Declaring a port "closed" when it is actually filtered (firewall dropped the packet) - a connect scanner cannot distinguish these well.
+- Scanning without a worker cap and melting the target or your own network.
+- Scanning anything you do not own or have written permission for.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `connect_ex` + timeout is the scanner primitive; loop it over a parsed port range.
+- argparse, resolution checks, and aligned output turn the loop into a tool.
+- Thread pools add speed; bounded concurrency keeps you polite and un-flagged.
+

+for port in found:
+    print(f"{port:>5}/tcp open  {SERVICES.get(port, 'unknown')}")
+print(f"{len(found)} open ports in {elapsed:.1f}s")
+```

+# Parsing Authentication Logs with Regex
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.

+- Extract structured data from messy log lines with regular expressions.

 ## Introduction
 
-Sample format: `timestamp,severity,source_ip,event`. Parse line by line, count severity and IP occurrences, count failed logins, and skip malformed records. This example summarizes supplied data; it is not a complete intrusion detection system.
+Module 09 built an auth-log analyzer with string splitting. Real logs are messier: usernames with spaces, IPv6, "invalid user" variants. **Regular expressions** match the *pattern* of a line instead of its exact layout, and Python's `re` module makes them practical.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Regex Essentials for Logs
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```python
+import re
+
+line = "Oct 10 03:14:15 srv sshd[812]: Failed password for invalid user admin from 203.0.113.7 port 51122 ssh2"
+
+ip = re.search(r"\d{1,3}(?:\.\d{1,3}){3}", line)
+print(ip.group())    # 203.0.113.7
+
+match = re.search(r"Failed password for (?:invalid user )?(\S+) from (\S+)", line)
+print(match.group(1), match.group(2))   # admin 203.0.113.7
+```
+
+The vocabulary that covers log work:
 
-## Syntax and Example
+- `\d` digit, `\w` word char, `\s` whitespace; `\S` non-space. `{1,3}` repeats, `+` one or more, `*` zero or more.
+- `(...)` captures a group; `(?:...)` groups without capturing; `?` makes the previous piece optional.
+- Raw strings (`r"..."`) keep backslashes literal - always use them for regex.
+- `re.search` finds the first match anywhere; `re.findall` returns all matches; `re.fullmatch` requires the whole string to match.
+
+Compile once when the pattern runs in a hot loop: `FAIL = re.compile(r"...")` then `FAIL.search(line)`.
+
+## From Lines to a Report
 
 ```python
+import re
 from collections import Counter
-from pathlib import Path
-
-def parse_log(path):
-    severities, ips = Counter(), Counter()
-    failed = malformed = 0

-    return severities, ips, failed, malformed
+
+FAIL = re.compile(r"Failed password for (?:invalid user )?(\S+) from (\S+)")
+
+def analyze(path):
+    users, ips = Counter(), Counter()
+    total = 0
+    with open(path, encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as f:
+        for line in f:
+            match = FAIL.search(line)
+            if not match:
+                continue
+            total += 1
+            user, ip = match.groups()
+            users[user] += 1
+            ips[ip] += 1
+    return total, users, ips
+
+total, users, ips = analyze("auth.log")
+print(f"{total} failed logins")
+print("\nTop source IPs:")
+for ip, n in ips.most_common(5):
+    print(f"  {ip:<16} {n}")
+print("\nTop targeted users:")
+for user, n in users.most_common(5):
+    print(f"  {user:<16} {n}")
 ```

 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Forgetting raw strings, so `\d` becomes an escape-sequence accident.
+- Greedy `.*` swallowing more than intended; prefer specific classes (`\S+`) or non-greedy `.*?`.
+- Assuming every line matches; always handle `match is None`.
+- Recompiling the pattern inside the loop.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Regex matches shape, not layout; `re.search` + capture groups extract fields from irregular lines.
+- Compile patterns once, use raw strings, and always handle non-matching lines.
+- `Counter.most_common` turns extracted fields into a ranked report in one line.

-# Safe Automation
+# Automating Security Tasks
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Turn repeated manual checks into scheduled, logged scripts.
+- Compare current state against a known-good baseline.
+- Report only what changed - the core trick of useful automation.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Start with read-only inventory. Use `pathlib` to enumerate files, `Counter` to count extensions, and `hashlib.sha256()` to hash bytes in chunks. Backups should refuse to overwrite existing destinations by default.
+The highest-value security scripts are rarely clever. They are the boring checks - has this file changed, are these the right users, did new services appear - run **every day without fail** and quiet unless something is wrong. This lesson builds the pattern: snapshot, baseline, diff, report.
 
-## Core Concepts
-
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
-
-## Syntax and Example
+## Pattern: Snapshot, Baseline, Diff
 
 ```python
 import hashlib
+import json
 from pathlib import Path
 
-def sha256_file(path):
-    digest = hashlib.sha256()
-    with Path(path).open("rb") as file:
-        for chunk in iter(lambda: file.read(65536), b""):
-            digest.update(chunk)
-    return digest.hexdigest()
+def snapshot(paths):
+    """Map each file path to its SHA-256 hex digest."""
+    state = {}
+    for path in paths:
+        p = Path(path)
+        if p.is_file():
+            state[str(p)] = hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest()
+    return state
+
+BASELINE = Path("baseline.json")
+
+current = snapshot(["/etc/passwd", "/etc/ssh/sshd_config"])
+if not BASELINE.exists():
+    BASELINE.write_text(json.dumps(current, indent=2), encoding="utf-8")
+    print("baseline created - run again later to compare")
+else:
+    old = json.loads(BASELINE.read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
+    for path in sorted(set(old) | set(current)):
+        if path not in old:
+            print(f"NEW:      {path}")
+        elif path not in current:
+            print(f"REMOVED:  {path}")
+        elif old[path] != current[path]:
+            print(f"MODIFIED: {path}")
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+```text
+$ python3 watchfiles.py
+baseline created - run again later to compare
+$ python3 watchfiles.py        # after /etc/ssh/sshd_config changed
+MODIFIED: /etc/ssh/sshd_config
+```
 
-Inventory a disposable practice directory, count extensions, hash a file, and design a backup function that refuses to overwrite.
+The same shape works for users (`/etc/passwd` lines as a set), open ports (re-run the module-01 scanner and diff the sets), or installed packages (`dpkg -l` / `pip freeze` output). Set difference (`new - old`, `old - new`) answers "what appeared/disappeared" directly.
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+## Scheduling and Logging
+
+A check nobody runs is nothing. Cron runs it daily; logging (module 16) keeps the evidence:
+
+```text
+# crontab -e
+15 3 * * *  /home/you/venv/bin/python /home/you/watchfiles.py >> /home/you/watchfiles.log 2>&1
+```
+
+Design rules for unattended scripts:
+
+1. **Silence is success.** Print only changes or errors; a daily "all good" email trains everyone to ignore it. (A weekly heartbeat is fine.)
+2. **Fail loudly.** Catch exceptions at the top level and exit nonzero with a clear message, so cron mail or monitoring notices.
+3. **State lives in one place.** Keep the baseline beside the script, version it, and treat unexpected baseline changes as their own finding.
+4. **Timeouts everywhere.** An unattended script that hangs is an incident, not a check.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Diffing against a baseline that was never reviewed - you enshrined a compromised state as "good."
+- Alerting on everything, so real changes drown in noise.
+- No logging: the script fired at 3 AM, something happened, and nobody can reconstruct what.
+- Hardcoded absolute paths that break when the repo moves; anchor with `Path(__file__).parent`.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Snapshot state, store a reviewed baseline, and diff with set/dict operations; report only changes.
+- Hashing (SHA-256) detects content changes; set difference detects additions and removals.
+- Cron plus logging makes checks durable: quiet on success, loud and nonzero on failure.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Create a baseline for two files you own, modify one, rerun, and confirm only that file is reported.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```text
+$ python3 watchfiles.py
+baseline created - run again later to compare
+$ echo "# comment" >> ~/.bashrc   # (or any file in the list)
+$ python3 watchfiles.py
+MODIFIED: /home/you/.bashrc
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Rewrite the file snapshot as a *user* snapshot: read `/etc/passwd` usernames into a set, store as JSON, and report NEW/REMOVED users on later runs.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import json
+from pathlib import Path
+
+BASE = Path("users-baseline.json")
+current = {line.split(":")[0] for line in Path("/etc/passwd").read_text().splitlines()}
+
+if not BASE.exists():
+    BASE.write_text(json.dumps(sorted(current)), encoding="utf-8")
+    print("baseline created")
+else:
+    old = set(json.loads(BASE.read_text()))
+    for user in sorted(current - old):
+        print("NEW:", user)
+    for user in sorted(old - current):
+        print("REMOVED:", user)
+```
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Add top-level error handling to the diff script: any unexpected exception prints "check failed" to stderr and exits with code 1.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import sys
+
+def main():
+    ...  # snapshot/diff logic
+
+if __name__ == "__main__":
+    try:
+        main()
+    except Exception as error:
+        print(f"check failed: {error}", file=sys.stderr)
+        sys.exit(1)
+```
+
+The blanket catch is acceptable here because it is the last boundary, it logs, and it sets a failing exit code - not silent swallowing.
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why report only differences instead of full state?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Attention is the scarce resource. A report of only changes keeps alerts meaningful; daily full dumps train people to ignore the tool.
+</details>
+
+**What does SHA-256 give you that file size and mtime do not?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Content integrity: same-size edits and timestamp-preserving changes still flip the digest.
+</details>
+
+**Why should an unattended check exit nonzero on error?**
 
-## Further Practice
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-Modify the example, explain each line in your own words, and link the exercise to the next module.
+Schedulers and monitoring surface failures through exit codes; a script that "succeeds" while broken disables the check silently.
+</details>

 ## Learning Objectives

 ## Introduction
 
-Inspect one authorized endpoint. Display status and selected headers; handle timeout and connection errors; parse JSON only when appropriate. Do not include credential theft, session hijacking, or unauthorized scanning.
+HTTP response headers are a server's security posture in miniature. A handful of response headers shut down whole attack classes; their absence is a finding on any review. This lesson builds a small checker - again, run it only against sites you own or have permission to assess.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## The Headers That Matter
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+| Header | Defends against | Good value |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| `Strict-Transport-Security` | protocol downgrade, cookie theft over HTTP | `max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains` |
+| `Content-Security-Policy` | XSS, data injection | a defined policy, not `unsafe-inline` everywhere |
+| `X-Frame-Options` | clickjacking | `DENY` or `SAMEORIGIN` |
+| `X-Content-Type-Options` | MIME sniffing | `nosniff` |
+| `Referrer-Policy` | leaking URLs to third parties | `strict-origin-when-cross-origin` or stricter |
+| `Set-Cookie` flags | session theft | `Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite` |
 
-## Syntax and Example
+## The Checker

+    "strict-transport-security": "forces HTTPS",

+    "x-frame-options": "blocks framing (clickjacking defense)",
+    "x-content-type-options": "stops MIME sniffing",

+Cookie flags need their own pass over `Set-Cookie` values: `Secure` (HTTPS only), `HttpOnly` (invisible to JavaScript, blunting XSS session theft), and `SameSite` (CSRF resistance).
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Flagging a missing header as a critical vulnerability rather than a hardening gap; context and the app's actual behavior matter.
+- Following redirects without noticing - the final page's headers are not the initial response's (`response.history` shows the chain).
+- Hammering a site with repeated checks; cache results and be polite.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- HSTS, CSP, X-Frame-Options, and X-Content-Type-Options each close a specific attack class; absence is a reportable hardening gap.
+- Lowercase header names before lookup; HTTP headers are case-insensitive.
+- Check cookie flags (`Secure`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite`) and banner leakage alongside the headline headers.

+```python
+def main():
+    password = getpass.getpass("Password to evaluate: ")
+    results = evaluate(password)
+    passed = sum(results.values())       # bools sum as 1/0

-# File Hash Calculator
+# Project: File Hash Calculator
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Hash files of any size with chunked reading.
+- Verify downloads against published checksums.
+- Compare hashes safely and report matches clearly.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Calculate SHA-256 over raw file bytes. Stream chunks rather than loading an entire large file into memory. A digest comparison is useful only when the reference digest is trusted.
+A cryptographic hash (SHA-256 here) maps any input to a fixed 64-hex-digit digest; change one bit of a file and the digest is unrecognizably different. That makes hashing the standard answer to "did this file change or arrive intact?" - download verification, backup integrity, and the file-watching from module 17.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Chunked Hashing
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
-
-## Syntax and Example
+Reading a 4 GB ISO into memory to hash it is a self-own. Hash in 64 KB chunks:
 
 ```python
 import hashlib
-from pathlib import Path
 
-def sha256(path):
+def sha256_file(path, chunk_size=65536):
     digest = hashlib.sha256()
-    with Path(path).open("rb") as file:
-        for chunk in iter(lambda: file.read(65536), b""):
+    with open(path, "rb") as f:
+        while chunk := f.read(chunk_size):
             digest.update(chunk)
     return digest.hexdigest()
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+The walrus operator `:=` assigns `chunk` and tests it in one expression: `f.read` returns empty bytes at EOF, which is falsy, ending the loop. Constant memory, any file size.
+
+## Verifying a Download
+
+Vendors publish checksums next to downloads. Comparing what you got against what they published catches corruption and (on a trusted channel) tampering:
+
+```python
+import sys
+
+def main():
+    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
+        sys.exit(f"usage: {sys.argv[0]} FILE EXPECTED_SHA256")
+    _, path, expected = sys.argv
+    actual = sha256_file(path)
+    print(f"file:     {path}")
+    print(f"actual:   {actual}")
+    print(f"expected: {expected.lower()}")
+    if actual == expected.strip().lower():
+        print("MATCH - file is intact")
+        return 0
+    print("MISMATCH - do not trust this file")
+    return 1
+
+if __name__ == "__main__":
+    raise SystemExit(main())
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 verify.py ubuntu.iso 9f8c...d2
+file:     ubuntu.iso
+actual:   9f8c...d2
+expected: 9f8c...d2
+MATCH - file is intact
+```
+
+Normalize case and whitespace before comparing - published checksums vary in formatting. Exit code 0/1 lets scripts chain the check (`verify.py ... && sudo ./install.sh`).
 
-Hash a file, change one byte, and hash again. Test a missing path and a directory path.
+## Notes That Matter
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+- **MD5 and SHA-1 are broken for security purposes** (collisions are practical). Fine for accidental-corruption checks, never for tamper evidence. Default to SHA-256.
+- A checksum only proves integrity if the *published* value came over a trusted channel; a hash posted next to the download on the same compromised server proves little.
+- `hashlib` also offers `sha512`, `blake2b` (fast); `hmac` for keyed verification.
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Hashing the whole `read()` of a huge file instead of chunking.
+- Comparing digests case-sensitively or with trailing whitespace and reporting false mismatches.
+- Using MD5 "because the site gave an MD5" without understanding it detects accidents, not attackers.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `hashlib.sha256()` + `.update()` in a read loop hashes any file in constant memory.
+- Compare normalized digests, report MATCH/MISMATCH, and return a meaningful exit code.
+- SHA-256 for anything security-relevant; MD5/SHA-1 only for accident detection.
+
+## Practice Exercises
+
+**Exercise 1.** Hash a file with your script and with the system tool (`sha256sum` on Linux, `shasum -a 256` on macOS, `certutil -hashfile f SHA256` on Windows). Confirm they agree.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```text
+$ python3 verify.py report.pdf $(sha256sum report.pdf | cut -d' ' -f1)
+file:     report.pdf
+actual:   4c2f...9a
+expected: 4c2f...9a
+MATCH - file is intact
+```
+
+Agreement with the OS tool validates the chunked implementation.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 2.** Flip one byte in a copy of the file (`printf 'x' | dd of=copy bs=1 count=1 conv=notrunc` on Linux/macOS) and show the digest changes completely.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```text
+$ cp report.pdf copy.pdf
+$ printf 'x' | dd of=copy.pdf bs=1 count=1 conv=notrunc
+$ python3 verify.py copy.pdf 4c2f...9a
+actual:   71bd...03
+MISMATCH - do not trust this file
+```
+
+The avalanche effect: one flipped bit changes about half the digest bits.
+</details>
+
+**Exercise 3.** Extend the script to hash every file in a directory and print a `sha256sum`-compatible listing (`digest  filename`).
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python

+
+for p in sorted(Path(sys.argv[1].rstrip('/')).glob("*")):
+    if p.is_file():
+        print(f"{sha256_file(p)}  {p.name}")
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 hashall.py downloads
+4c2f...9a  report.pdf
+aa01...7e  notes.txt
+```
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why hash in chunks instead of `sha256(path.read_bytes())`?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`read_bytes()` loads the whole file into RAM; chunked updates keep memory constant regardless of file size.
+</details>
+
+**What does the walrus operator do in the read loop?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+`while chunk := f.read(n)` assigns the read result to `chunk` and tests its truthiness in one step, ending the loop at EOF (empty bytes).
+</details>
+
+**Why is MD5 unacceptable for verifying software against tampering?**
 
-## Further Practice
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
 
-Modify the example, explain each line in your own words, and link the exercise to the next module.
+Practical collision attacks let an attacker craft a malicious file with the same MD5 as the genuine one; SHA-256 has no such practical attacks.
+</details>

+        "failed_logins": sum(1 for e in events if e.get("path") == "/login" and e.get("status") == 401),
+    }

 ```python

+## Concurrent Checks and a Table
+
+```python
+from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
+
+URLS = ["https://example.com", "https://httpbin.org/status/503", "https://no-such-host.invalid"]
+
+with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=10) as pool:
+    results = list(pool.map(check, URLS))
+
+print(f"{'URL':<38} {'STATUS':>6} {'MS':>7}  RESULT")
+for r in results:
+    verdict = "OK" if r["ok"] else (r["error"] or "HTTP " + str(r["status"]))
+    print(f"{r['url']:<38} {str(r['status']):>6} {r['ms']:>7}  {verdict}")
+```

-# Inventory and Backup Utility
+# Project: Directory Inventory and Backup
 
 ## Learning Objectives
 
-- Understand the purpose and vocabulary of this topic.
-- Read and modify the examples.
-- Apply the concept in a small independent exercise.
+- Inventory a directory tree: sizes, types, and newest/oldest files.
+- Create timestamped, verified backups of important files.
+- Combine pathlib, hashing, and reporting into one maintenance tool.
 
 ## Introduction
 
-Inventory should be read-only. A backup utility should validate the source, refuse an existing destination, create parent directories when appropriate, and copy rather than move or delete the original.
+The final project is the one you will actually run monthly: inventory a directory (what is here, how big, what changed) and back up the important parts with integrity verification. It stitches together pathlib (module 10), hashing (project 2), JSON state (project 3), and clean reporting.
 
-## Core Concepts
+## Part 1: Inventory
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.
+```python
+from pathlib import Path
 
-## Syntax and Example
+def inventory(root):
+    files = [p for p in Path(root).rglob("*") if p.is_file()]
+    total = sum(p.stat().st_size for p in files)
+    by_suffix = {}
+    for p in files:
+        by_suffix.setdefault(p.suffix.lower() or "(none)", []).append(p)
+    print(f"{len(files)} files, {total / 1e6:.1f} MB under {root}")
+    for suffix, group in sorted(by_suffix.items(), key=lambda kv: -len(kv[1]))[:8]:
+        size = sum(p.stat().st_size for p in group)
+        print(f"  {suffix:>8} {len(group):>5} files  {size / 1e6:>8.1f} MB")
+    newest = max(files, key=lambda p: p.stat().st_mtime, default=None)
+    if newest:
+        print("newest:", newest)
+```
+
+```text
+$ python3 inventory.py ~/Documents
+412 files, 86.3 MB under /home/you/Documents
+       .pdf   102 files      61.2 MB
+      .docx    87 files      18.4 MB
+       .png    55 files       4.1 MB
+newest: /home/you/Documents/notes/todo.md
+```
+
+`rglob("*")` walks the tree; grouping by suffix with a dict of lists (module 08 pattern) shows where the space goes.
+
+## Part 2: Verified Backup
+
+Copy important files to a timestamped folder, then prove every copy matches its source:
 
 ```python
+import hashlib
 import shutil
+from datetime import datetime
 from pathlib import Path
 
-def backup(source, destination):
-    src, dst = Path(source), Path(destination)
-    if not src.is_file():
-        raise FileNotFoundError(src)
-    if dst.exists():
-        raise FileExistsError(dst)
-    dst.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
-    shutil.copy2(src, dst)
-    return dst
+def digest(path):
+    h = hashlib.sha256()
+    with open(path, "rb") as f:
+        while chunk := f.read(65536):
+            h.update(chunk)
+    return h.hexdigest()
+
+def backup(sources, dest_root):
+    stamp = datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d-%H%M%S")
+    dest = Path(dest_root) / f"backup-{stamp}"
+    copied, failed = 0, 0
+    for source in sources:
+        src = Path(source)
+        if not src.is_file():
+            print(f"SKIP (missing): {src}")
+            continue
+        target = dest / src.name
+        target.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
+        shutil.copy2(src, target)              # preserves timestamps
+        if digest(src) == digest(target):
+            copied += 1
+        else:
+            failed += 1
+            print(f"CORRUPT COPY: {src}")
+    print(f"{copied} files backed up to {dest}" + (f", {failed} FAILED" if failed else ""))
+
+backup(["/etc/ssh/sshd_config", str(Path.home() / ".bashrc")], Path.home() / "backups")
 ```
 
-## Practical Exercise
+Verification is the difference between a backup and a pile of copies: hash source and destination immediately after copying. `shutil.copy2` preserves modification times, which later inventory runs will thank you for.
 
-Add confirmation for user-selected destinations, test overwrite refusal, and compare source and backup hashes.
+## Notes That Matter
 
-**Hints**
-1. Write down the expected input and output first.
-2. Break the task into small steps or functions.
-3. Test a normal case, a boundary case, and invalid input where relevant.
+- This is a *local* backup. Real backup doctrine is 3-2-1: three copies, two media, one off-site - this script is one building block.
+- For large trees, `rsync -a --delete src/ dest/` does this better; knowing what it must do underneath makes you a competent user of it.
+- Deduplication idea: name backup files by content hash so unchanged files never get stored twice (that is how real backup tools work).
 
 ## Common Mistakes
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+- Copying without verifying - silent truncation and disk errors turn backups into decor.
+- Timestamped folders with local time that collide across timezones; UTC or fine-grained stamps avoid it.
+- Recursively backing up the backup destination into itself. Exclude the destination path.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- `rglob` + `stat()` + grouping answers "what is in this tree and how big is it."
+- Verified copies (hash source and destination) make backups trustworthy; `shutil.copy2` preserves metadata.
+- Timestamped destinations plus skip/fail reporting make the tool safe to run unattended.

+**Exercise 3.** Store a `manifest.json` inside each backup folder mapping filename to digest, then write the verify function that re-hashes the backup and reports OK/CHANGED per file.
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show a worked solution</summary>
+
+```python
+import json
+
+manifest = {t.name: digest(t) for t in dest.glob("*") if t.is_file()}
+(dest / "manifest.json").write_text(json.dumps(manifest, indent=2), encoding="utf-8")
+
+def verify(backup_dir):
+    backup_dir = Path(backup_dir)
+    manifest = json.loads((backup_dir / "manifest.json").read_text())
+    for name, expected in manifest.items():
+        actual = digest(backup_dir / name)
+        print(f"{'OK     ' if actual == expected else 'CHANGED'} {name}")
+```
+</details>
+
+## Check Yourself
+
+**Why hash the copy after `shutil.copy2`?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+To catch write-time corruption (disk errors, truncation, interruption) immediately, instead of discovering it when restoring.
+</details>
+
+**What does `p.suffix.lower() or "(none)"` handle?**
+
+<details>
+<summary>Show answer</summary>
+
+Files with no extension produce an empty suffix string; the `or` groups them under a readable label, and lowercasing merges `.JPG` with `.jpg`.
+</details>
+

+## The Map
 
-Work through the explanation before running the example. Identify the input, the operation performed, and the output or side effect. Python is case-sensitive; indentation defines blocks; values have types; and errors should be handled at the point where the program can respond meaningfully.

+## Ten Lines You Should Be Able to Write From Memory

+```python
+for line in open(path, encoding="utf-8"): ...           # stream a file
+counts[key] = counts.get(key, 0) + 1                     # count anything
+sorted(d.items(), key=lambda kv: kv[1], reverse=True)    # rank by value
+try:
+    risky_call()
+except SpecificError:                        # targeted handling
+if __name__ == "__main__": main()                        # script structure
+requests.get(url, timeout=5)                             # never without timeout
+subprocess.run([cmd, arg], capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=60)
+socket.create_connection((host, port), timeout=3)        # raw TCP
+hashlib.sha256(p.read_bytes()).hexdigest()               # small-file hash
+assert f(x) == expected                                  # always be testing
+```
+
+If any line is shaky, that module is your next hour of practice.

+from collections import Counter
+
+counts = Counter()

+```

+

+    with socket.socket() as s:
+        s.settimeout(0.5)
+        if s.connect_ex((host, port)) == 0:
+            print(port, "open")
+```
+</details>

+## How to Read Official Documentation
 
-## Common Mistakes
+The Python docs are the authority - tutorials (including this course) simplify; the docs define. A practical approach:
 
-- Copying code without understanding the values and types.
-- Assuming input, files, or network services are always available.
-- Ignoring error messages or catching every exception indiscriminately.
-- Forgetting resource cleanup or safe authorization boundaries.
+1. **Start at the module's top page.** The first paragraphs state the purpose and the two or three functions everyone needs. For `socket`, that is `create_connection` and the socket families.
+2. **Use the tutorial for learning, the library reference for answers.** The tutorial (link above) teaches; the library reference is for "what are this function's parameters exactly?"
+3. **Check the version.** Docs pages have a version selector; behavior and available functions differ between 3.8 and 3.13. Match it to your interpreter (`python3 --version`).
+4. **Search within the page** for the method name; module pages are long by design.
+
+When a third-party answer (Stack Overflow, a blog) disagrees with the docs, the docs win. When the docs and your interpreter disagree, your interpreter is right about *your* machine - recheck versions.
+
+## What to Learn Next
+
+In rough order of payoff for security-oriented scripting:
+
+1. **Testing with `pytest`** - real test files, fixtures, and reports. Your assert matrices already transfer.
+2. **argparse in depth** - subcommands (`tool scan ...`, `tool report ...`), which is how professional CLIs are shaped.
+3. **The `logging` module's handlers and files** - rotating logs for long-running tools.
+4. **Secure configuration** - keeping tokens in environment variables or files with strict permissions, never in code.
+5. **Deeper networking** - threading versus asyncio for concurrent I/O, and the scapy/pwntools ecosystems when you are ready for packet crafting and exploit development.
+6. **Object-oriented Python** - classes, when a tool outgrows functions and dicts.
+
+## A Sustainable Practice Loop
+
+- One small finished tool per week beats five abandoned ones.
+- Keep a `scripts/` directory of everything you build; re-reading your own code in three months is the cheapest progress meter there is.
+- Practice platforms (OverTheWire, PicoCTF, TryHackMe) supply legal, structured problems when your own ideas run dry.
 
 ## Key Takeaways
 
-- Choose the simplest construct that clearly expresses the task.
-- Validate assumptions at program boundaries.
-- Test the behavior, not merely whether the program starts.
+- Official docs are the authority; match their version to your interpreter.
+- Next skills: pytest, argparse subcommands, logging handlers, secure config, concurrency, then OOP.
+- Small finished tools on a steady cadence are the practice that sticks.
